\mathbf{D}_{\varphi}^{\ast}\!\big(e^{i\varphi}\mathbf{T}\big)=e^{i\varphi}\,\mathbf{D}^{\ast}\mathbf{T}\qquad\text{in }\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Omega),

which follows immediately from the product rule and
\nabla(e^{i\varphi})=ie^{i\varphi}\nabla\varphi
. Consequently,

\mathcal{L}_{\varphi,\omega}\mathbf{w}=e^{i\varphi}\Big(-\nabla\cdot\big(2\mu_{0}\,\mathbf{D}(\mathbf{w})\big)+i\mu_{0}\,\nabla\cdot\big(\mathcal{S}_{\varphi}[\mathbf{w}]\big)\Big)+i\omega\,\rho\,\mathbf{w}\qquad\text{in }V_{\sigma,\varphi}^{\ast},

(4.48)

where
\mathcal{S}_{\varphi}[\mathbf{w}]=\mathbf{w}\otimes\nabla\varphi+\nabla\varphi\otimes\mathbf{w}
.

The decomposition (
4.48
) isolates the texture-induced contributions beyond the constant-coefficient Stokes core:

1.

The
zeroth-order complex multiplier

e^{i\varphi(\mathbf{x})}
, which is harmless if
\varphi
is constant but cannot be removed globally
when
\varphi
varies (it does not commute with the solenoidal constraint/projection).

2.

A
first-order commutator/drift mechanism
encoded by

\,i\mu_{0}e^{i\varphi}\nabla\cdot(\mathcal{S}_{\varphi}[\mathbf{w}])
,
which is linear in
\nabla\varphi
and contains exactly one derivative of
\mathbf{w}
(plus lower-order pieces under further expansion).

3.

The unchanged oscillatory mass term
i\omega\rho\,\mathbf{w}
.

Thus,
even with
|\mu^{*}|=\mu_{0}
spatially uniform
, spatial variation of
\varphi
produces explicit lower-order structure controlled by

\nabla\varphi
and (after enforcing the transported incompressibility constraint) yields a genuinely non-selfadjoint/non-normal perturbation of the
baseline viscous dynamics; this is the operator-level manifestation of the phase-gradient mechanism developed earlier

[
19
,
25
,
35
]
.

If one additionally assumes
\varphi\in W^{2,\infty}(\Omega)
(or
\varphi\in C^{1,1}
) and
\mathbf{w}\in H^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{C}^{d})
, then

\nabla\cdot(\mathcal{S}_{\varphi}[\mathbf{w}])
can be expanded a.e. as

\nabla\cdot(\mathcal{S}_{\varphi}[\mathbf{w}])=(\nabla\varphi\cdot\nabla)\mathbf{w}+(\nabla\cdot\mathbf{w})\,\nabla\varphi+(\Delta\varphi)\,\mathbf{w}+\big(\mathrm{Hess}\,\varphi\big)\,\mathbf{w},

(4.49)

where
(\mathrm{Hess}\,\varphi)\,\mathbf{w}
denotes the matrix–vector product of the Hessian with
\mathbf{w}
.
Using the transported incompressibility constraint
\nabla\cdot\mathbf{w}=i\nabla\varphi\cdot\mathbf{w}
from
Lemma
4.9
, one may rewrite (
4.48
) pointwise in terms of
\mathbf{w}
,
\nabla\varphi

and
\nabla^{2}\varphi
(with the rank-one term
(\nabla\cdot\mathbf{w})\nabla\varphi=i(\nabla\varphi\cdot\mathbf{w})\nabla\varphi
made explicit),
clarifying the drift- and potential-like components at the PDE level.

For readers who prefer a constraint-enforced PDE rather than the projected operator, one may equivalently write a
saddle-point system on the full space: find
(\mathbf{w},\pi)
such that

\mathcal{L}_{\varphi,\omega}\mathbf{w}+\nabla\pi=\mathbf{f}\quad\text{in }\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Omega),\qquad\nabla\cdot(e^{-i\varphi}\mathbf{w})=0\quad\text{in }\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Omega),\qquad\mathbf{w}|_{\Gamma_{D}}=0,

(4.50)

with
\pi
interpreted as the Lagrange multiplier enforcing the pullback constraint and
\mathbf{f}
interpreted in the appropriate dual space
(e.g.
V_{\sigma,\varphi}^{\ast}
). Projecting (
4.50
) onto
H_{\sigma,\varphi}
recovers (
4.47
);
see standard saddle-point formulations for Stokes-type systems
[
13
,
2
,
1
]
.

Remark 4.11
(From the covariant strong-form representative back to the form decomposition
\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}_{\omega}=\mathfrak{a}^{(0)}_{\omega}+\mathfrak{b}_{\varphi}
)
.

The distributional representative
\mathcal{L}_{\varphi,\omega}
(whether written in the compact covariant form

\,2\mu_{0}\,\mathbf{D}_{\varphi}^{\ast}\mathbf{D}_{\varphi}+i\omega M_{\rho}
or in an expanded divergence/drift form) is only a
realization device
:
the operator
\widetilde{A}_{\omega}
is determined uniquely by the closed sectorial form

\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}_{\omega}(\mathbf{w},\mathbf{z})=\int_{\Omega}2\mu_{0}\,\mathbf{D}_{\varphi}(\mathbf{w}):\overline{\mathbf{D}_{\varphi}(\mathbf{z})}\,d\mathbf{x}\;+\;i\omega\int_{\Omega}\rho\,\mathbf{w}\cdot\overline{\mathbf{z}}\,d\mathbf{x},\qquad\mathbf{w},\mathbf{z}\in V_{\sigma,\varphi}.

Accordingly, any two distributional expressions for the “strong form” that differ by an element of
V_{\sigma,\varphi}^{\ast}
annihilating

V_{\sigma,\varphi}
test functions (e.g. a boundary-supported distribution, or a gradient term absorbed by the saddle-point pressure/Lagrange multiplier)
induce the same realized operator on
H_{\sigma,\varphi}
via the form method.

To connect directly with the perturbation-theoretic decomposition used in
(
4.23
)–(
4.25
), expand

\mathbf{D}_{\varphi}=\mathbf{D}-\frac{i}{2}\mathcal{S}_{\varphi}
inside the viscous term:

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}2\mu_{0}\,\mathbf{D}_{\varphi}(\mathbf{w}):\overline{\mathbf{D}_{\varphi}(\mathbf{z})}\,d\mathbf{x}

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}2\mu_{0}\,\mathbf{D}(\mathbf{w}):\overline{\mathbf{D}(\mathbf{z})}\,d\mathbf{x}

(4.51)

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{\Omega}\Big[i\mu_{0}\,\mathbf{D}(\mathbf{w}):\overline{\mathcal{S}_{\varphi}[\mathbf{z}]}-i\mu_{0}\,\mathcal{S}_{\varphi}[\mathbf{w}]:\overline{\mathbf{D}(\mathbf{z})}+\frac{\mu_{0}}{2}\,\mathcal{S}_{\varphi}[\mathbf{w}]:\overline{\mathcal{S}_{\varphi}[\mathbf{z}]}\Big]\,d\mathbf{x}.

(4.52)

Therefore,

\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}_{\omega}(\mathbf{w},\mathbf{z})=\mathfrak{a}^{(0)}_{\omega}(\mathbf{w},\mathbf{z})+\mathfrak{b}_{\varphi}(\mathbf{w},\mathbf{z}),\qquad\mathbf{w},\mathbf{z}\in V_{\sigma,\varphi},

with
\mathfrak{a}^{(0)}_{\omega}
and
\mathfrak{b}_{\varphi}
exactly as defined in (
4.25
). In particular, the drift/commutator pieces visible in the expanded formula are precisely the integration-by-parts realizations of the cross terms in
\mathfrak{b}_{\varphi}
, while the

\frac{\mu_{0}}{2}\int_{\Omega}\mathcal{S}_{\varphi}[\mathbf{w}]:\overline{\mathcal{S}_{\varphi}[\mathbf{z}]}\,d\mathbf{x}
contribution is the associated
zeroth-order (potential-type) component at the form level.

Consequently, in the sense of
form sums
(KLMN), the compensated operator satisfies

\widetilde{A}_{\omega}=A^{(0)}_{\omega}\ \dotplus\ \mathcal{B}_{\varphi},\qquad\langle\mathcal{B}_{\varphi}\mathbf{w},\mathbf{z}\rangle_{V_{\sigma,\varphi}^{\ast},V_{\sigma,\varphi}}:=\mathfrak{b}_{\varphi}(\mathbf{w},\mathbf{z}),

where
\mathcal{B}_{\varphi}:V_{\sigma,\varphi}\to V_{\sigma,\varphi}^{\ast}
is the bounded perturbation induced by
\mathfrak{b}_{\varphi}
.
By Lemma
4.7
,
\mathfrak{b}_{\varphi}
is
\mathfrak{a}^{(0)}_{\omega}
-bounded with arbitrarily small relative bound (at the expense
of an
L^{2}
term), hence
\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}_{\omega}
is closed and sectorial and generates
\widetilde{A}_{\omega}
.

Moreover, for any
\lambda\in\rho(A^{(0)}_{\omega})
such that the bounded operator

K_{\varphi}(\lambda):=\mathcal{B}_{\varphi}(A^{(0)}_{\omega}-\lambda I)^{-1}\in\mathcal{L}(H_{\sigma,\varphi})
satisfies

\|K_{\varphi}(\lambda)\|<1
, one has the resolvent identity

(\widetilde{A}_{\omega}-\lambda I)^{-1}=(A^{(0)}_{\omega}-\lambda I)^{-1}\big(I+K_{\varphi}(\lambda)\big)^{-1},

and the corresponding perturbation bound

\|(\widetilde{A}_{\omega}-\lambda I)^{-1}-(A^{(0)}_{\omega}-\lambda I)^{-1}\|\leq\frac{\|(A^{(0)}_{\omega}-\lambda I)^{-1}\|^{2}\,\|\mathcal{B}_{\varphi}\|}{1-\|K_{\varphi}(\lambda)\|}.

We refer to standard treatments of sectorial forms, KLMN, and resolvent perturbation theory for details

[
19
,
35
,
25
]
.

5
Passivity-Consistent Impedance and a Positivity Statement

The (complex) hydraulic impedance is defined by

Z^{*}(\omega):=\frac{\widehat{\Delta P}(\omega)}{\hat{Q}(\omega)},

which generalizes the steady hydraulic resistance and, in the classical Newtonian constant-viscosity setting,
reduces to the familiar Womersley-type frequency response in canonical pipe/channel geometries

[
38
,
39
]
.
In linear time-harmonic regimes one writes

Z^{*}(\omega)=R(\omega)+iX(\omega),

where the
resistive
part
R(\omega)=\real Z^{*}(\omega)
controls cycle-averaged dissipation, while the
reactive
part

X(\omega)=\imaginary Z^{*}(\omega)
encodes reversible energy storage and phase lag between pressure drop and flux (inertia and/or constitutive
reactance).

The novelty emphasized here is not the existence of an impedance observable per se, but the fact that
constitutive phase textures

(i.e. spatial variation of
\arg\mu^{*}(\mathbf{x},\omega)
at essentially fixed
|\mu^{*}|
) can drive
macroscopic phase anomalies

in
\arg Z^{*}(\omega)
even under strict passivity. In particular:

•

Passivity at the constitutive level (
\real\mu^{*}\geq 0
) enforces a nonnegativity constraint on the
resistive
part
\real Z^{*}(\omega)

•

There is
no
analogous sign restriction on
\imaginary Z^{*}(\omega)
or on
\arg Z^{*}(\omega)
; hence large, frequency-localized phase excursions
can occur without violating dissipativity.

•

In the phase-only class
\mu^{*}=\mu_{0}e^{i\varphi(\mathbf{x},\omega)}
, one can modulate the reactive response through
\imaginary\mu^{*}
while leaving
the dissipation scale controlled by
\real\mu^{*}=\mu_{0}\cos\varphi
, thereby separating “phase effects” from “magnitude-only heterogeneity.”

This section formalizes the positivity constraint on
\real Z^{*}
and makes explicit, at the level of complex power identities, why strong
impedance-phase anomalies are compatible with passivity.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
(
d\in\{2,3\}
) be a bounded Lipschitz truncation of a pressure-driven geometry (channel/pipe/BFS cavity).
Assume the boundary decomposes as

\partial\Omega=\Gamma_{w}\ \dot{\cup}\ \Sigma_{\mathrm{in}}\ \dot{\cup}\ \Sigma_{\mathrm{out}},

where
\Gamma_{w}
is the rigid wall boundary and
\Sigma_{\mathrm{in}}
,
\Sigma_{\mathrm{out}}
are inlet/outlet stations.
We impose homogeneous no-slip on the wall,

\hat{\mathbf{v}}=0\quad\text{on }\Gamma_{w},

and impose pressure-drop forcing through normal traction data on
\Sigma_{\mathrm{in}}
and
\Sigma_{\mathrm{out}}
.
A convenient idealization (consistent with many laboratory and numerical station definitions) is that the imposed traction is purely normal
and spatially constant on each station:

\big(-\hat{p}\,I+2\mu^{*}(\mathbf{x},\omega)\mathbf{D}(\hat{\mathbf{v}})\big)\mathbf{n}=-\hat{p}_{\mathrm{in}}\,\mathbf{n}\quad\text{on }\Sigma_{\mathrm{in}},\qquad\big(-\hat{p}\,I+2\mu^{*}(\mathbf{x},\omega)\mathbf{D}(\hat{\mathbf{v}})\big)\mathbf{n}=-\hat{p}_{\mathrm{out}}\,\mathbf{n}\quad\text{on }\Sigma_{\mathrm{out}},

(5.1)

with complex scalars
\hat{p}_{\mathrm{in}},\hat{p}_{\mathrm{out}}
and outward unit normal
\mathbf{n}
.
Define the complex pressure drop

\widehat{\Delta P}(\omega):=\hat{p}_{\mathrm{in}}(\omega)-\hat{p}_{\mathrm{out}}(\omega).

Define the complex volumetric flux through a station
\Sigma
by

\hat{Q}(\omega):=\int_{\Sigma}\hat{\mathbf{v}}(\mathbf{x};\omega)\cdot\mathbf{n}\,dS.

By incompressibility and impermeable walls,

\int_{\Sigma_{\mathrm{out}}}\hat{\mathbf{v}}\cdot\mathbf{n}\,dS=-\int_{\Sigma_{\mathrm{in}}}\hat{\mathbf{v}}\cdot\mathbf{n}\,dS,

so it is consistent to set

\hat{Q}:=\int_{\Sigma_{\mathrm{out}}}\hat{\mathbf{v}}\cdot\mathbf{n}\,dS\quad\Rightarrow\quad\int_{\Sigma_{\mathrm{in}}}\hat{\mathbf{v}}\cdot\mathbf{n}\,dS=-\hat{Q}.

Definition 5.1

(Complex impedance)
.

Whenever
\hat{Q}(\omega)\neq 0
, define the complex impedance by

Z^{*}(\omega):=\frac{\widehat{\Delta P}(\omega)}{\hat{Q}(\omega)}.

(5.2)

Remark 5.1
(Gauge invariance: dependence on pressure
drop
rather than absolute pressure)
.

The definition (
5.2
) is invariant under the pressure gauge transformation

\hat{p}\mapsto\hat{p}+c
for any complex constant
c
, since only
\widehat{\Delta P}
enters.
This aligns with the PDE structure: pressure is a Lagrange multiplier enforcing incompressibility and is determined only up to an additive constant.

Remark 5.2
(Complex power, average dissipation, and the operational meaning of
\real Z^{*}
)
.

With the phasor convention
\Delta P(t)=\real\{\widehat{\Delta P}\,e^{i\omega t}\}
and
Q(t)=\real\{\hat{Q}\,e^{i\omega t}\}
,
the cycle-averaged power input is

\langle P_{\mathrm{in}}\rangle=\frac{1}{2}\,\real\{\widehat{\Delta P}\,\overline{\hat{Q}}\}.

Thus
\real\{\widehat{\Delta P}\,\overline{\hat{Q}}\}
is the correct quantity to compare against viscous dissipation, and

\real Z^{*}(\omega)=\frac{\real\{\widehat{\Delta P}\,\overline{\hat{Q}}\}}{|\hat{Q}|^{2}}

is the effective resistance per unit
|Q|^{2}
(the direct analog of
R=\Delta P/Q
in steady laminar flows).

We now show that constitutive passivity forces
\real Z^{*}(\omega)\geq 0
in pressure-driven settings, and we record the complementary reactive identity
that governs
\imaginary Z^{*}(\omega)
.

Proposition 5.1

(Complex power identity and nonnegativity of
\real Z^{*}
under passivity)
.

Let
(\hat{\mathbf{v}},\hat{p})
be a (weak) solution of the harmonic Stokes system

i\omega\rho\,\hat{\mathbf{v}}=-\nabla\hat{p}+\nabla\cdot\big(2\mu^{*}(\mathbf{x},\omega)\mathbf{D}(\hat{\mathbf{v}})\big),\qquad\nabla\cdot\hat{\mathbf{v}}=0\quad\text{in }\Omega,

(5.3)

with homogeneous no-slip on
\Gamma_{w}
and traction boundary conditions (
5.1
) on

\Sigma_{\mathrm{in}}\cup\Sigma_{\mathrm{out}}
.
Assume
\rho\in L^{\infty}(\Omega)
with
\rho>0
a.e. and
\mu^{*}(\cdot,\omega)\in L^{\infty}(\Omega;\mathbb{C})
with

\real\mu^{*}(\mathbf{x},\omega)\geq 0\quad\text{a.e.\ in }\Omega.

(5.4)

Then the following complex power identity holds:

\widehat{\Delta P}\,\overline{\hat{Q}}=i\omega\int_{\Omega}\rho\,|\hat{\mathbf{v}}|^{2}\,d\mathbf{x}+\int_{\Omega}2\,\mu^{*}(\mathbf{x},\omega)\,|\mathbf{D}(\hat{\mathbf{v}})|^{2}\,d\mathbf{x}.

(5.5)

Consequently,

\displaystyle\real\{\widehat{\Delta P}\,\overline{\hat{Q}}\}

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}2\,\real\mu^{*}(\mathbf{x},\omega)\,|\mathbf{D}(\hat{\mathbf{v}})|^{2}\,d\mathbf{x}\geq 0,

(5.6)

\displaystyle\imaginary\{\widehat{\Delta P}\,\overline{\hat{Q}}\}

\displaystyle=\omega\int_{\Omega}\rho\,|\hat{\mathbf{v}}|^{2}\,d\mathbf{x}+\int_{\Omega}2\,\imaginary\mu^{*}(\mathbf{x},\omega)\,|\mathbf{D}(\hat{\mathbf{v}})|^{2}\,d\mathbf{x}.

(5.7)

In particular, whenever
\hat{Q}\neq 0
,

\real Z^{*}(\omega)=\frac{\real\{\widehat{\Delta P}\,\overline{\hat{Q}}\}}{|\hat{Q}|^{2}}=\frac{1}{|\hat{Q}|^{2}}\int_{\Omega}2\,\real\mu^{*}(\mathbf{x},\omega)\,|\mathbf{D}(\hat{\mathbf{v}})|^{2}\,d\mathbf{x}\geq 0.

(5.8)

Moreover, if
\real\mu^{*}(\mathbf{x},\omega)\geq\mu_{\min}>0
a.e., then
\real Z^{*}(\omega)>0
whenever
\hat{Q}(\omega)\neq 0
.

Proof.

Multiply (
5.3
) by
\overline{\hat{\mathbf{v}}}
and integrate over
\Omega
:

i\omega\int_{\Omega}\rho\,|\hat{\mathbf{v}}|^{2}\,d\mathbf{x}=-\int_{\Omega}\nabla\hat{p}\cdot\overline{\hat{\mathbf{v}}}\,d\mathbf{x}+\int_{\Omega}\nabla\cdot\big(2\mu^{*}\mathbf{D}(\hat{\mathbf{v}})\big)\cdot\overline{\hat{\mathbf{v}}}\,d\mathbf{x}.

For the pressure term, use incompressibility and integration by parts in the weak sense:

-\int_{\Omega}\nabla\hat{p}\cdot\overline{\hat{\mathbf{v}}}\,d\mathbf{x}=-\int_{\partial\Omega}\hat{p}\,\overline{\hat{\mathbf{v}}}\cdot\mathbf{n}\,dS,

since
\int_{\Omega}\hat{p}\,\overline{\nabla\cdot\hat{\mathbf{v}}}\,d\mathbf{x}=0
.
For the viscous term, apply the symmetric-gradient Green identity:

\int_{\Omega}\nabla\cdot\big(2\mu^{*}\mathbf{D}(\hat{\mathbf{v}})\big)\cdot\overline{\hat{\mathbf{v}}}\,d\mathbf{x}=-\int_{\Omega}2\mu^{*}\,|\mathbf{D}(\hat{\mathbf{v}})|^{2}\,d\mathbf{x}+\int_{\partial\Omega}\big(2\mu^{*}\mathbf{D}(\hat{\mathbf{v}})\mathbf{n}\big)\cdot\overline{\hat{\mathbf{v}}}\,dS.

Combining boundary contributions yields

\int_{\partial\Omega}\big(-\hat{p}\,I+2\mu^{*}\mathbf{D}(\hat{\mathbf{v}})\big)\mathbf{n}\cdot\overline{\hat{\mathbf{v}}}\,dS.

On
\Gamma_{w}
we have
\hat{\mathbf{v}}=0
, so the wall contributes no power. On
\Sigma_{\mathrm{in}}
and
\Sigma_{\mathrm{out}}
,
substitute (
5.1
) to obtain

\displaystyle\int_{\Sigma_{\mathrm{in}}\cup\Sigma_{\mathrm{out}}}\big(-\hat{p}\,I+2\mu^{*}\mathbf{D}(\hat{\mathbf{v}})\big)\mathbf{n}\cdot\overline{\hat{\mathbf{v}}}\,dS

\displaystyle=-\hat{p}_{\mathrm{in}}\int_{\Sigma_{\mathrm{in}}}\overline{\hat{\mathbf{v}}}\cdot\mathbf{n}\,dS-\hat{p}_{\mathrm{out}}\int_{\Sigma_{\mathrm{out}}}\overline{\hat{\mathbf{v}}}\cdot\mathbf{n}\,dS

\displaystyle=\hat{p}_{\mathrm{in}}\,\overline{\hat{Q}}-\hat{p}_{\mathrm{out}}\,\overline{\hat{Q}}=\widehat{\Delta P}\,\overline{\hat{Q}},

using
\int_{\Sigma_{\mathrm{in}}}\hat{\mathbf{v}}\cdot\mathbf{n}\,dS=-\hat{Q}
and
\int_{\Sigma_{\mathrm{out}}}\hat{\mathbf{v}}\cdot\mathbf{n}\,dS=\hat{Q}
.
Substituting this boundary identity into the integrated momentum balance yields (
5.5
).
Taking real parts gives (
5.6
) since
\real\{i\omega\int_{\Omega}\rho|\hat{\mathbf{v}}|^{2}\}=0
,
and taking imaginary parts yields (
5.7
). Dividing (
5.6
) by
|\hat{Q}|^{2}

gives (
5.8
). For strict positivity under
\real\mu^{*}\geq\mu_{\min}>0
, note that if the dissipation integral vanishes then

\mathbf{D}(\hat{\mathbf{v}})=0
a.e., hence
\hat{\mathbf{v}}=0
by Korn plus the wall Dirichlet constraint, which forces
\hat{Q}=0
.
∎

Remark 5.3
(A precise pointwise “positive-real” statement for the impedance)
.

Identity (
5.8
) is the exact pointwise-in-
\omega
analog of the positive-real constraint for passive impedances:
for pressure-driven forcing, the mapping
\widehat{\Delta P}\mapsto\hat{Q}
defines a complex admittance

Y^{*}(\omega):=\hat{Q}(\omega)/\widehat{\Delta P}(\omega)
, and passivity forbids negative resistive part (net energy extraction.)

Remark 5.4
(Why phase textures can produce large
\arg Z^{*}
without changing dissipation magnitude)
.

The formula (
5.8
) shows that
\real Z^{*}
is a dissipation-to-flux quotient:

\real Z^{*}(\omega)=\frac{\int_{\Omega}2\real\mu^{*}\,|\mathbf{D}(\hat{\mathbf{v}})|^{2}}{|\hat{Q}|^{2}}.

Thus, for fixed
\real\mu^{*}
(in particular in the phase-only class where
\real\mu^{*}=\mu_{0}\cos\varphi
can be kept nearly uniform),
a substantial change in
\arg Z^{*}
must come through changes in the
complex
relationship between
\widehat{\Delta P}
and
\hat{Q}
,
i.e. through the reactive balance (
5.7
) and through spatial reorganization of
|\mathbf{D}(\hat{\mathbf{v}})|
.
This is precisely where phase textures act: via intrinsic non-normality and texture-gradient forcing, they can localize strain/vorticity
and alter the phase of
\hat{Q}
relative to
\widehat{\Delta P}
without requiring any increase in the dissipative integrand.

Remark 5.5
(Connection to canonical oscillatory-flow physics)
.

In a Newtonian fluid with real viscosity,
\imaginary\mu^{*}\equiv 0
and (
5.7
) reduces to

\imaginary\{\widehat{\Delta P}\,\overline{\hat{Q}}\}=\omega\int_{\Omega}\rho\,|\hat{\mathbf{v}}|^{2},

so the reactive part is purely inertial and matches the classical Womersley picture
[
38
,
39
]
.
In a viscoelastic/structured medium with complex
\mu^{*}
, the additional term

\int_{\Omega}2\,\imaginary\mu^{*}\,|\mathbf{D}(\hat{\mathbf{v}})|^{2}
supplies a constitutive reactance, which can either reinforce or oppose inertial reactance
depending on the sign of
\imaginary\mu^{*}
.
Spatially varying phase textures can amplify this effect by redistributing the strain field toward regions where
\imaginary\mu^{*}
is locally large in magnitude
(or where the resolvent selects strong strain localization), thereby producing sharp, frequency-localized phase anomalies in
\arg Z^{*}
.

Remark 5.6
(A macroscopic signature tied to operator non-normality)
.

The point of Proposition
5.1
is not merely that
\real Z^{*}\geq 0
(a standard passivity statement),
but that it isolates a clean experimental/numerical diagnostic: if a phase-only texture (essentially fixed
|\mu^{*}|
) produces large excursions in

\arg Z^{*}(\omega)
while leaving
\real Z^{*}(\omega)
comparable to the baseline, then the mechanism cannot be attributed to magnitude-only heterogeneous
dissipation. Instead, it must come from the phase-sensitive operator structure (intrinsic non-normality and texture-gradient forcing) that reorganizes
the strain/vorticity fields and hence the complex flux response.

6
3D Periodic Textures: Toeplitz/Laurent Structure and Truncation Stability

Spanwise-periodic constitutive textures generate
linear mode coupling
in the
z
-Fourier representation.
On product domains
\Omega=\Omega_{2D}\times(0,L_{z})
this yields a bi-infinite block Toeplitz/Laurent system in the Fourier index,
i.e. an operator on sequences of
(x,y)
-fields whose coefficients depend only on index differences.
This viewpoint provides: (i) a clean functional-analytic formulation of the coupled system; (ii) a principled justification for finite-band
and finite-section truncations with explicit operator-norm error bounds; and (iii) a transparent linear “sideband generation” mechanism linking
spanwise pattern selection to resolvent amplification (cf. Floquet/Bragg-type scattering in periodic media
[
28
]
).

We assume a product geometry

\Omega=\Omega_{2D}\times(0,L_{z}),\qquad\Omega_{2D}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}\ \text{bounded Lipschitz},

with
z
-periodicity of period
L_{z}
and wall boundary conditions (e.g. no-slip) on
\partial\Omega_{2D}\times(0,L_{z})
.
Let
\kappa_{k}:=2\pi k/L_{z}
and write
\mathbf{x}=(\mathbf{x}_{\perp},z)
with
\mathbf{x}_{\perp}=(x,y)
.
For fixed forcing frequency
\omega>0
, expand

\mu^{*}(\mathbf{x}_{\perp},z;\omega)=\sum_{\ell\in\mathbb{Z}}\widehat{\mu}_{\ell}(\mathbf{x}_{\perp};\omega)\,e^{i\kappa_{\ell}z},\qquad\hat{\mathbf{v}}(\mathbf{x}_{\perp},z;\omega)=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathbf{u}_{k}(\mathbf{x}_{\perp};\omega)\,e^{i\kappa_{k}z}.

(6.1)

We suppress
\omega
henceforth. Use the orthonormal basis
e_{k}(z):=L_{z}^{-1/2}e^{i\kappa_{k}z}
in
L^{2}(0,L_{z})
.
Then the Fourier map in
z
is unitary:

\mathcal{F}_{z}:L^{2}\big((0,L_{z});X\big)\to\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z};X),\qquad f=\sum_{k}f_{k}e_{k}\ \mapsto\ \{f_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{Z}},

and Parseval reads
\|f\|_{L^{2}((0,L_{z});X)}^{2}=\sum_{k}\|f_{k}\|_{X}^{2}
.

Modewise incompressibility (3D periodic reduction).

Write
\mathbf{u}_{k}=(\mathbf{u}_{\perp,k},u_{z,k})
with
\mathbf{u}_{\perp,k}:\Omega_{2D}\to\mathbb{C}^{2}
and
u_{z,k}:\Omega_{2D}\to\mathbb{C}
.
The
k
th Fourier coefficient of
\nabla\cdot\hat{\mathbf{v}}=0
is the
twisted divergence constraint

\nabla_{\perp}\cdot\mathbf{u}_{\perp,k}+i\kappa_{k}\,u_{z,k}=0\qquad\text{in }\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Omega_{2D}),\ \ k\in\mathbb{Z}.

(6.2)

Accordingly, define the ambient transverse spaces

H_{2D}:=L^{2}(\Omega_{2D};\mathbb{C}^{3}),\qquad V_{2D}:=\{\mathbf{u}\in H^{1}(\Omega_{2D};\mathbb{C}^{3}):\mathbf{u}|_{\partial\Omega_{2D}}=0\},

and the modewise solenoidal subspaces

H_{\sigma,k}:=\{\mathbf{u}\in H_{2D}:\nabla_{\perp}\cdot\mathbf{u}_{\perp}+i\kappa_{k}u_{z}=0\text{ in }\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Omega_{2D})\},\quad V_{\sigma,k}:=H_{\sigma,k}\cap V_{2D}.

The natural sequence spaces are the Hilbert direct sums

\mathbb{H}:=\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z};H_{2D}),\qquad\mathbb{V}:=\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z};V_{2D}),\qquad\mathbb{H}_{\sigma}:=\bigoplus_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}H_{\sigma,k},\qquad\mathbb{V}_{\sigma}:=\bigoplus_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}V_{\sigma,k}.

For
s\in\mathbb{R}
define

\mathbb{H}^{(s)}:=\Big\{\mathbf{u}\in\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z};H_{2D}):\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}(1+|\kappa_{k}|^{2})^{s}\|\mathbf{u}_{k}\|_{H_{2D}}^{2}<\infty\Big\},

(6.3)

with the natural norm (and similarly
\mathbb{H}^{(s)}_{\sigma}:=\mathbb{H}^{(s)}\cap\mathbb{H}_{\sigma}
).

Lemma 6.1

(Fourier isometries in the periodic direction)
.

Let
X
be a Hilbert space. With the normalization above,
\mathcal{F}_{z}
is unitary.
Moreover, for
s\in\mathbb{R}
,

f\in H^{s}\big((0,L_{z});X\big)\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\{f_{k}\}\in\mathbb{H}^{(s)}\ \text{with weight }(1+|\kappa_{k}|^{2})^{s/2},

and the norms coincide under consistent normalization.

Remark 6.1
(Why weighted spaces are structural)
.

The
z
-derivatives in
\mathbf{D}(\hat{\mathbf{v}})
and
\nabla\cdot(2\mu^{*}\mathbf{D}(\hat{\mathbf{v}}))
produce factors of
\kappa_{k}
on the

k
th Fourier mode. Weighted sequence norms are the natural setting in which these diagonal Fourier multipliers become bounded/closed operators and
in which Toeplitz coupling estimates can be stated without ambiguity.

6.0.1
Laurent (Toeplitz) Coupling From Coefficient Multiplication

In
z
-Fourier variables, multiplication by
\mu^{*}(\mathbf{x}_{\perp},z)
is represented by the Laurent (bi-infinite Toeplitz) convolution

(\mathbb{M}_{\mu}\mathbf{w})_{k}:=\sum_{\ell\in\mathbb{Z}}\widehat{\mu}_{\ell}(\mathbf{x}_{\perp})\,\mathbf{w}_{k-\ell},\qquad k\in\mathbb{Z}.

(6.4)

Equivalently,
\mathbb{M}_{\mu}=\mathcal{F}_{z}(\mu^{*}\cdot)\mathcal{F}_{z}^{-1}
on
\mathbb{H}
.

Remark 6.2
(Solenoidality is not preserved by coefficient multiplication)
.

In general,
\mathbb{M}_{\mu}
(or individual multipliers
\widehat{\mu}_{\ell}(\mathbf{x}_{\perp})
) does
not
map
\mathbb{H}_{\sigma}
into

\mathbb{H}_{\sigma}
unless the coefficient is transverse-constant: the constraint (
6.2
) is not invariant under

\mathbf{u}\mapsto\widehat{\mu}_{\ell}(\mathbf{x}_{\perp})\mathbf{u}
because
\nabla_{\perp}\widehat{\mu}_{\ell}
enters upon taking divergence.
For pressure-eliminated realizations the correct solenoidal object is therefore
P_{\sigma}\mathbb{M}_{\mu}
(or modewise
P_{\sigma,k}\mathbb{M}_{\mu}
),
which remains bounded on
\mathbb{H}
.

Lemma 6.2

(Boundedness of
\mathbb{M}_{\mu}
; Wiener algebra refinement)
.

Assume
\mu^{*}\in L^{\infty}\big(\Omega_{2D}\times(0,L_{z})\big)
.
Then
\mathbb{M}_{\mu}
is bounded on
\mathbb{H}
with

\|\mathbb{M}_{\mu}\|_{\mathcal{L}(\mathbb{H},\mathbb{H})}\leq\|\mu^{*}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega_{2D}\times(0,L_{z}))}.

If, in addition, the stronger Wiener-type condition holds:

\sum_{\ell\in\mathbb{Z}}\|\widehat{\mu}_{\ell}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega_{2D})}<\infty,

(6.5)

then
\mathbb{M}_{\mu}
admits the explicit estimate

\|\mathbb{M}_{\mu}\|_{\mathcal{L}(\mathbb{H},\mathbb{H})}\leq\sum_{\ell\in\mathbb{Z}}\|\widehat{\mu}_{\ell}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega_{2D})},

and the finite-band truncation

\mathbb{M}^{(M)}_{\mu}\mathbf{w}:=\{\sum_{|\ell|\leq M}\widehat{\mu}_{\ell}\,\mathbf{w}_{k-\ell}\}_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}

satisfies

\|\mathbb{M}_{\mu}-\mathbb{M}^{(M)}_{\mu}\|_{\mathcal{L}(\mathbb{H},\mathbb{H})}\leq\sum_{|\ell|>M}\|\widehat{\mu}_{\ell}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega_{2D})}.

(6.6)

More generally, if
\sum_{\ell}(1+|\kappa_{\ell}|)^{r}\|\widehat{\mu}_{\ell}\|_{L^{\infty}}<\infty
for some
r\geq 0
, then
\mathbb{M}_{\mu}
is bounded on

\mathbb{H}^{(s)}
for all
0\leq s\leq r
(discrete product-rule control in
z
).

Proof.

The
L^{\infty}
bound is immediate from the unitary equivalence
\mathbb{M}_{\mu}=\mathcal{F}_{z}(\mu^{*}\cdot)\mathcal{F}_{z}^{-1}
on
\mathbb{H}
.
Under (
6.5
), the explicit estimate follows from the discrete
\ell^{1}
–
\ell^{2}
Young inequality applied pointwise in
\mathbf{x}_{\perp}
:
with
a_{\ell}:=\|\widehat{\mu}_{\ell}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega_{2D})}
and
b_{k}:=\|\mathbf{w}_{k}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{2D})}
,

\|(\mathbb{M}_{\mu}\mathbf{w})_{k}\|_{L^{2}}\leq\sum_{\ell\in\mathbb{Z}}a_{\ell}\,b_{k-\ell}\quad\Rightarrow\quad\|\mathbb{M}_{\mu}\mathbf{w}\|_{\ell^{2}(L^{2})}\leq\|a*b\|_{\ell^{2}}\leq\|a\|_{\ell^{1}}\|b\|_{\ell^{2}}.

The truncation estimate (
6.6
) follows by applying the same bound to the tail kernel

\{a_{\ell}\}_{|\ell|>M}
.
The weighted statement follows by commuting
(1+|\kappa_{k}|)^{s}
through convolution using

(1+|\kappa_{k}|)^{s}\lesssim(1+|\kappa_{k-\ell}|)^{s}(1+|\kappa_{\ell}|)^{s}
and repeating the Young estimate.
∎

6.0.2
Toeplitz Structure of the Viscous Operator

Let
\mathbf{D}_{\kappa}
denote the symmetric gradient in
\Omega
evaluated on a single
z
-Fourier mode with parameter
\kappa

(i.e.
\partial_{z}
acts as multiplication by
i\kappa
).
Then the viscous stress
2\mu^{*}\mathbf{D}(\hat{\mathbf{v}})
has
k
th Fourier coefficient

\widehat{(2\mu^{*}\mathbf{D}(\hat{\mathbf{v}}))}_{k}=2\sum_{\ell\in\mathbb{Z}}\widehat{\mu}_{\ell}\,\mathbf{D}_{\kappa_{k-\ell}}(\mathbf{u}_{k-\ell}),

and applying divergence preserves Toeplitz coupling in the coefficient index (with additional transverse commutators involving
\nabla_{\perp}\widehat{\mu}_{\ell}
).
Abstractly, the viscous mapping can be written as a bi-infinite block Toeplitz/Laurent operator

(\mathbb{T}\mathbf{u})_{k}:=\sum_{\ell\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathcal{A}_{\ell}^{(k)}[\mathbf{u}_{k-\ell}],\qquad k\in\mathbb{Z},

(6.7)

where each block
\mathcal{A}_{\ell}^{(k)}
is an
(x,y)
-operator built from
\widehat{\mu}_{\ell}
(and its transverse derivatives) together with

\mathbf{D}_{\kappa_{k-\ell}}
and the transverse divergence. Two complementary viewpoints are useful (see, e.g., Toeplitz/Laurent operator treatments in periodic settings
[
24
]

•

Laurent Viewpoint (Coefficient Coupling.)

The only source of inter-mode coupling is multiplication by
\mu^{*}
, i.e.
\mathbb{M}_{\mu}
.
All
z
-derivative contributions appear as diagonal Fourier multipliers in
k
.

•

Toeplitz-Plus-Diagonal-Multipliers Viewpoint (Full-Operator Algebra.)

The full viscous operator is an algebraic combination of
\mathbb{M}_{\mu}
, shifts on
\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
, and the diagonal multiplier

\mathbb{K}
defined by
(\mathbb{K}\mathbf{u})_{k}=\kappa_{k}\mathbf{u}_{k}
, with commutation relations such as

\mathbb{K}\mathbb{S}=\mathbb{S}(\mathbb{K}+\kappa_{1})
for the unit shift
\mathbb{S}
.

To keep subsequent truncation statements both correct and usable, we proceed by bounding
\mathbb{T}
directly in the relevant operator norms.
Assume the blocks satisfy the uniform coefficient-to-operator bound: there exists
C_{\Omega}>0
such that

\|\mathcal{A}_{\ell}^{(k)}\|_{\mathcal{L}(V_{2D},V_{2D}^{*})}\leq C_{\Omega}\,\|\widehat{\mu}_{\ell}\|_{W^{1,\infty}(\Omega_{2D})}\,\big(1+|\kappa_{k}|+|\kappa_{k-\ell}|\big)^{m},\qquad\forall k,\ell\in\mathbb{Z},

(6.8)

where
m\in\{0,1\}
depends on whether the chosen norm tracks one
z
-derivative (e.g.
m=1
for
H^{1}
-in-
z
control,
m=0
for purely
L^{2}
-level coupling).
Under (
6.8
) and the summability conditions in Lemma
6.2
,
\mathbb{T}
is bounded

\mathbb{V}\to\mathbb{V}^{*}
(and similarly on the corresponding weighted spaces), and the same tail-sum mechanism yields explicit finite-band truncation error bounds.

6.1
Explicit Coercivity/Continuity Constants For the Oscillatory Stokes/Oseen Toeplitz System.

Baseline block form and natural constants.

Fix
\omega>0
. Assume the density satisfies
0<\rho_{\min}\leq\rho\leq\rho_{\max}
a.e. in
\Omega_{2D}
.
Assume the
z
-mean coefficient obeys the (Tier I) passivity/accretivity bounds

0<\mu_{\min}\leq\real\widehat{\mu}_{0}(x,y)\quad\text{and}\quad|\widehat{\mu}_{0}(x,y)|\leq\mu_{\max}\qquad\text{a.e.\ in }\Omega_{2D}.

(6.9)

Let
\mathcal{L}^{(0)}_{\omega}(\kappa_{k}):V_{\sigma,2D}\to V_{\sigma,2D}^{*}
denote the
k
th block of the baseline operator
(oscillatory Stokes, or Oseen with
z
-independent base flow). The induced block-diagonal operator on sequences is

(\mathbb{L}^{(0)}_{\omega,\mathrm{lin}}\mathbf{u})_{k}:=\mathcal{L}^{(0)}_{\omega}(\kappa_{k})\mathbf{u}_{k}.

Equivalently,
\mathbb{L}^{(0)}_{\omega,\mathrm{lin}}
is represented by a bounded sesquilinear form

\mathfrak{a}^{(0)}_{\omega}:\mathbb{V}_{\sigma}\times\mathbb{V}_{\sigma}\to\mathbb{C}
via

\langle\mathbb{L}^{(0)}_{\omega,\mathrm{lin}}\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}\rangle:=\mathfrak{a}^{(0)}_{\omega}(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v})
,
with

\mathfrak{a}^{(0)}_{\omega}(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}):=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\int_{\Omega_{2D}}2\widehat{\mu}_{0}\,\mathbf{D}_{\kappa_{k}}(\mathbf{u}_{k}):\overline{\mathbf{D}_{\kappa_{k}}(\mathbf{v}_{k})}\,d(x,y)\;+\;i\omega\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\int_{\Omega_{2D}}\rho\,\mathbf{u}_{k}\cdot\overline{\mathbf{v}_{k}}\,d(x,y).

(6.10)

(Here
\mathbf{D}_{\kappa_{k}}
is the symmetric gradient augmented by the Fourier parameter
\kappa_{k}
; in particular,
\partial_{z}
becomes
i\kappa_{k}
.)

Remark 6.3
(Norm conventions)
.

For the constants below to be
uniform in
k
, the canonical choice is to work in a
z
-weighted space (e.g.
\mathbb{V}_{\sigma}^{(1)}
),
or to build the
\kappa_{k}
-dependence directly into the mode norm. Concretely, one may take

\|\mathbf{u}\|_{\mathbb{V}_{\sigma,\kappa}}^{2}:=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\|\mathbf{D}_{\kappa_{k}}(\mathbf{u}_{k})\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{2D})}^{2},

which is equivalent to the usual
H^{1}
-type norm on the periodic product domain under Korn/Poincaré for no-slip walls.
If you prefer to keep
\mathbb{V}_{\sigma}=\ell^{2}(H^{1}_{xy})
unweighted, then the same estimates hold
on each finite section

|k|\leq K

with constants that may depend on
K
through
\max_{|k|\leq K}|\kappa_{k}|
.

Lemma 6.3

(Explicit continuity and ellipticity constants for the baseline form)
.

Assume (
6.9
). Let
C_{P}
and
C_{K}
denote the Poincaré and Korn constants on the cross-section
(no-slip on
\partial\Omega_{2D}
), so that

\|\mathbf{w}\|_{L^{2}}\leq C_{P}\|\nabla\mathbf{w}\|_{L^{2}}
and
\|\nabla\mathbf{w}\|_{L^{2}}\leq C_{K}\|\mathbf{D}(\mathbf{w})\|_{L^{2}}
for admissible
\mathbf{w}
.
Set
C_{PK}:=C_{P}C_{K}
.
Then the baseline form (
6.10
) is continuous and elliptic in the real part on
\mathbb{V}_{\sigma,\kappa}
:

\displaystyle|\mathfrak{a}^{(0)}_{\omega}(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v})|

\displaystyle\leq C_{0}(\omega)\,\|\mathbf{u}\|_{\mathbb{V}_{\sigma,\kappa}}\,\|\mathbf{v}\|_{\mathbb{V}_{\sigma,\kappa}},

(6.11)

\displaystyle\real\,\mathfrak{a}^{(0)}_{\omega}(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{u})

\displaystyle\geq\alpha_{0}\,\|\mathbf{u}\|_{\mathbb{V}_{\sigma,\kappa}}^{2},

(6.12)

with the explicit constants

\alpha_{0}:=2\mu_{\min},\qquad C_{0}(\omega):=2\mu_{\max}+\omega\rho_{\max}\,C_{PK}^{2}.

(6.13)

Proof.

For the real part, the oscillatory mass term is purely imaginary, hence

\real\,\mathfrak{a}^{(0)}_{\omega}(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{u})=\sum_{k}\int 2\real\widehat{\mu}_{0}\,|\mathbf{D}_{\kappa_{k}}(\mathbf{u}_{k})|^{2}\geq 2\mu_{\min}\sum_{k}\|\mathbf{D}_{\kappa_{k}}(\mathbf{u}_{k})\|_{L^{2}}^{2}=\alpha_{0}\|\mathbf{u}\|_{\mathbb{V}_{\sigma,\kappa}}^{2},

giving (
6.12
).
For continuity, apply Cauchy–Schwarz to the viscous term and use
|\widehat{\mu}_{0}|\leq\mu_{\max}
:

\Big|\sum_{k}\int 2\widehat{\mu}_{0}\,\mathbf{D}_{\kappa_{k}}(\mathbf{u}_{k}):\overline{\mathbf{D}_{\kappa_{k}}(\mathbf{v}_{k})}\Big|\leq 2\mu_{\max}\|\mathbf{u}\|_{\mathbb{V}_{\sigma,\kappa}}\|\mathbf{v}\|_{\mathbb{V}_{\sigma,\kappa}}.

For the mass term, use
\rho\leq\rho_{\max}
and Poincaré+Korn to control
\|\mathbf{u}_{k}\|_{L^{2}}
by
\|\mathbf{D}_{\kappa_{k}}(\mathbf{u}_{k})\|_{L^{2}}

(with constant
C_{PK}
at the level of the cross-section), giving

\omega\Big|\sum_{k}\int\rho\,\mathbf{u}_{k}\cdot\overline{\mathbf{v}_{k}}\Big|\leq\omega\rho_{\max}\sum_{k}\|\mathbf{u}_{k}\|_{L^{2}}\|\mathbf{v}_{k}\|_{L^{2}}\leq\omega\rho_{\max}C_{PK}^{2}\,\|\mathbf{u}\|_{\mathbb{V}_{\sigma,\kappa}}\|\mathbf{v}\|_{\mathbb{V}_{\sigma,\kappa}},

which yields (
6.11
) with (
6.13
).
∎

Remark 6.4
(Optional: adding bounded Oseen advection)
.

If the baseline blocks include a
z
-independent Oseen term (e.g.
\mathbf{V}_{0}\cdot\nabla
with
\mathbf{V}_{0}\in W^{1,\infty}
),
it contributes a bounded form on
\mathbb{V}_{\sigma,\kappa}
and simply adds to
C_{0}(\omega)
by a term of size

\lesssim\|\mathbf{V}_{0}\|_{W^{1,\infty}}
(standard lower-order perturbation theory for sectorial forms; see
[
19
]
).
The real-part ellipticity constant
\alpha_{0}
is unchanged provided the added term is not strongly accretive-negative.

Full Toeplitz-coupled operator: explicit
\alpha_{L},C_{L}
.

Let
\mathbb{T}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathbb{V}_{\sigma,\kappa},\mathbb{V}_{\sigma,\kappa}^{*})
be the Toeplitz/Laurent coupling from
earlier, and define

\mathbb{L}_{\omega,\mathrm{lin}}:=\mathbb{L}^{(0)}_{\omega,\mathrm{lin}}+\mathbb{T},\qquad\mathfrak{a}_{\omega}:=\mathfrak{a}^{(0)}_{\omega}+\mathfrak{t},\qquad\langle\mathbb{T}\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}\rangle:=\mathfrak{t}(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}).

Set
\tau:=\|\mathbb{T}\|_{\mathcal{L}(\mathbb{V}_{\sigma,\kappa},\mathbb{V}_{\sigma,\kappa}^{*})}
.

Lemma 6.4

(Continuity and ellipticity constants for
\mathbb{L}_{\omega,\mathrm{lin}}
)
.

Under Lemma
6.3
and
\mathbb{T}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathbb{V}_{\sigma,\kappa},\mathbb{V}_{\sigma,\kappa}^{*})
,
the full form
\mathfrak{a}_{\omega}
is continuous with

|\mathfrak{a}_{\omega}(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v})|\leq C_{L}(\omega)\,\|\mathbf{u}\|_{\mathbb{V}_{\sigma,\kappa}}\|\mathbf{v}\|_{\mathbb{V}_{\sigma,\kappa}},\qquad C_{L}(\omega):=C_{0}(\omega)+\tau.

(6.14)

Moreover,
\mathfrak{a}_{\omega}
is elliptic in the real part provided
\tau<\alpha_{0}
, and then

\real\,\mathfrak{a}_{\omega}(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{u})\geq\alpha_{L}\,\|\mathbf{u}\|_{\mathbb{V}_{\sigma,\kappa}}^{2},\qquad\alpha_{L}:=\alpha_{0}-\tau=2\mu_{\min}-\|\mathbb{T}\|.

(6.15)

Proof.

Continuity is immediate from Lemma
6.3
and the definition of
\tau
.
For ellipticity,

\real\,\mathfrak{a}_{\omega}(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{u})=\real\,\mathfrak{a}^{(0)}_{\omega}(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{u})+\real\,\langle\mathbb{T}\mathbf{u},\mathbf{u}\rangle\geq\alpha_{0}\|\mathbf{u}\|^{2}-|\langle\mathbb{T}\mathbf{u},\mathbf{u}\rangle|\geq(\alpha_{0}-\tau)\|\mathbf{u}\|^{2}.

∎

Remark 6.5
(Connection to the Neumann-series resolvent condition)
.

Lemma
6.4
is the
form-level
route to stability (uniform ellipticity in real part).
Your earlier perturbative invertibility condition
M_{0}(\omega)\|\mathbb{T}\|<1
is an
operator-level
route and does not require sign control on

\real\langle\mathbb{T}\mathbf{u},\mathbf{u}\rangle
.
When both apply, one obtains complementary bounds:
\|(\mathbb{L}^{(0)})^{-1}\|\leq 1/\alpha_{0}
by Lax–Milgram, hence

M_{0}(\omega)\|\mathbb{T}\|<1
is implied by
\|\mathbb{T}\|<\alpha_{0}
but can be weaker/sharper depending on the normed setting.
See
[
19
]
for the general form/operator correspondence.

6.1.1
Combined Truncation Pipeline: Texture-Band Truncation + Response Galerkin Truncation.

Step 1: texture-band truncation.

Let
\mathbb{T}^{(N)}
be the
N
-band coefficient truncation and define

\mathbb{L}^{(N)}_{\omega,\mathrm{lin}}:=\mathbb{L}^{(0)}_{\omega,\mathrm{lin}}+\mathbb{T}^{(N)}.

Set
\tau_{N}:=\|\mathbb{T}^{(N)}\|
and
\delta_{N}:=\|\mathbb{T}-\mathbb{T}^{(N)}\|
.
Note
\tau_{N}\leq\tau
and
\delta_{N}\to 0
.

Proposition 6.1

(Texture truncation error (operator-norm controlled))
.

Assume Lemma
6.3
and
\tau<\alpha_{0}
so that
\alpha_{L}>0
.
Let
\mathbf{u}
and
\mathbf{u}^{(N)}
solve

\mathbb{L}_{\omega,\mathrm{lin}}\mathbf{u}=\mathbf{f},\qquad\mathbb{L}^{(N)}_{\omega,\mathrm{lin}}\mathbf{u}^{(N)}=\mathbf{f}\quad\text{in }\mathbb{V}_{\sigma,\kappa}^{*},

with
\mathbf{f}\in\mathbb{V}_{\sigma,\kappa}^{*}
.
Then

\|\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{u}^{(N)}\|_{\mathbb{V}_{\sigma,\kappa}}\leq\frac{\delta_{N}}{\alpha_{L}\,\alpha_{L}^{(N)}}\,\|\mathbf{f}\|_{\mathbb{V}_{\sigma,\kappa}^{*}},\qquad\alpha_{L}^{(N)}:=\alpha_{0}-\tau_{N}\ \geq\ \alpha_{L},

(6.16)

and in particular
\|\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{u}^{(N)}\|\lesssim\delta_{N}\,\|\mathbf{f}\|
with an explicit constant depending only on

\mu_{\min}
and
\|\mathbb{T}\|
.

Proof.

Subtract the equations to obtain

\mathbb{L}_{\omega,\mathrm{lin}}(\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{u}^{(N)})=(\mathbb{T}^{(N)}-\mathbb{T})\mathbf{u}^{(N)}.

By ellipticity of
\mathbb{L}_{\omega,\mathrm{lin}}
(Lemma
6.4
),

\|\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{u}^{(N)}\|\leq\alpha_{L}^{-1}\|(\mathbb{T}-\mathbb{T}^{(N)})\mathbf{u}^{(N)}\|_{*}\leq\alpha_{L}^{-1}\delta_{N}\|\mathbf{u}^{(N)}\|
.
By ellipticity of
\mathbb{L}^{(N)}_{\omega,\mathrm{lin}}
with constant
\alpha_{L}^{(N)}
, we have

\|\mathbf{u}^{(N)}\|\leq(\alpha_{L}^{(N)})^{-1}\|\mathbf{f}\|_{*}
, giving (
6.16
).
∎

Step 2: response truncation as conforming Galerkin in
k
.

Let
\mathbb{P}_{K}
project onto
\{|k|\leq K\}
and set
\mathbb{V}^{[K]}_{\sigma,\kappa}:=\mathbb{P}_{K}\mathbb{V}_{\sigma,\kappa}
.
Define the Galerkin solution
\mathbf{u}^{[K,N]}\in\mathbb{V}^{[K]}_{\sigma,\kappa}
by

\langle\mathbb{L}^{(N)}_{\omega,\mathrm{lin}}\mathbf{u}^{[K,N]},\mathbf{v}\rangle=\langle\mathbf{f},\mathbf{v}\rangle\qquad\forall\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{V}^{[K]}_{\sigma,\kappa}.

Proposition 6.2

(Response truncation error and final combined estimate)
.

Assume
\tau<\alpha_{0}
.
Then the Galerkin problem defining
\mathbf{u}^{[K,N]}
is uniquely solvable and satisfies the quasi-optimal bound

\|\mathbf{u}^{(N)}-\mathbf{u}^{[K,N]}\|_{\mathbb{V}_{\sigma,\kappa}}\leq\frac{C_{L}^{(N)}(\omega)}{\alpha_{L}^{(N)}}\,\|(I-\mathbb{P}_{K})\mathbf{u}^{(N)}\|_{\mathbb{V}_{\sigma,\kappa}},\qquad C_{L}^{(N)}(\omega):=C_{0}(\omega)+\tau_{N},

(6.17)

(Céa for complex coercive forms
[
19
]
).
Consequently, the
full
approximation error splits as

\|\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{u}^{[K,N]}\|_{\mathbb{V}_{\sigma,\kappa}}\leq\underbrace{\frac{\delta_{N}}{\alpha_{L}\,\alpha_{L}^{(N)}}\,\|\mathbf{f}\|_{\mathbb{V}_{\sigma,\kappa}^{*}}}_{\text{texture (band) truncation}}\;+\;\underbrace{\frac{C_{L}^{(N)}(\omega)}{\alpha_{L}^{(N)}}\,\|(I-\mathbb{P}_{K})\mathbf{u}^{(N)}\|_{\mathbb{V}_{\sigma,\kappa}}}_{\text{response ($k$) truncation}}.

(6.18)

If additionally
\mathbf{u}^{(N)}\in\mathbb{V}_{\sigma,\kappa}^{(s)}
for some
s>0
, then

\|(I-\mathbb{P}_{K})\mathbf{u}^{(N)}\|_{\mathbb{V}_{\sigma,\kappa}}\leq(1+|\kappa_{K+1}|^{2})^{-s/2}\,\|\mathbf{u}^{(N)}\|_{\mathbb{V}_{\sigma,\kappa}^{(s)}},

(6.19)

so the response truncation rate is dictated by
z
-Sobolev regularity.

Remark 6.6
(Interpretation in non-normal regimes)
.

Estimate (
6.18
) isolates the two distinct numerical-modeling burdens in the Toeplitz setting:
(i) operator-norm control of omitted texture harmonics (
\delta_{N}
), and
(ii) decay of the true response Fourier tail in
k
(controlled by
\mathbb{V}^{(s)}
regularity).
In non-normal blocks, large resolvent norms can magnify both effects, hence quantitative tail control is materially more important than in
selfadjoint problems
[
9
]
.

7
Worked Examples.

The worked examples in this section serve two complementary roles.
Conceptually, they provide calibrated settings in which the paper’s operator-level mechanisms can be seen without interference from secondary effects
(geometry, corner singularities, separation, or nonlinear transport), so that the reader can develop reliable intuition for what the constitutive
textures do—and, equally importantly, what they do
not
do.
Computationally, they are designed as “reportable” benchmarks: each example is posed in a form that admits clean nondimensionalization, a transparent
passivity regime, and a small set of diagnostics that can be reproduced across discretizations and solvers.
In this way, the examples bridge the abstract mapping properties established earlier (coercivity, resolvent bounds, and texture-induced coupling operators)
and concrete signatures that are experimentally or numerically legible in classical fluid mechanics.

Across the sequence, the examples are organized to isolate mechanisms by progressively adding structure while keeping the governing problems linear and
well-posed.
We begin with canonical calibration problems (e.g. half-space oscillatory shear and impedance-type boundary observables) in which the classical
“single-wavenumber” picture is explicit, and then introduce phase-only and spanwise textures whose spatial variation changes the operator through
first-order couplings
[
45
,
46
]
.
Subsequent examples move to geometries and coefficient classes that generate explicit mode coupling (Toeplitz/Laurent structure under periodic textures),
enabling direct identification of sideband transfer, non-normal amplification, and frequency-localized phase anomalies that cannot be absorbed into any
spatially constant complex-viscosity surrogate.
Each worked example concludes with a small collection of plots and scalar metrics (chosen to be robust to numerical details) that summarize the mechanism
in a form suitable for comparison, parameter sweeps, and potential experimental interpretation.

7.1
Worked Example I: Stokes’ Second Problem with a Phase-Textured Wall Layer.

Stokes’ second problem is the canonical oscillatory boundary-layer calculation: an oscillating wall drives a shear wave into a half-space

[
45
,
46
,
47
]
.
For constant viscosity, the response is governed by a single complex wavenumber, hence by a single penetration depth and an affine-in-
y
phase profile.
We use this geometry as a calibration problem because it cleanly isolates
constitutive
effects from advection, corners, separation, and
pressure-driven complexities.

The principal message is that when complex viscosity is treated as a spatially resolved constitutive field
\mu^{*}(y,\omega)
, spatial modulation of its

argument

\varphi(y)=\arg\mu^{*}(y,\omega)
introduces an irreducible first-order coupling proportional to
\varphi^{\prime}(y)
.
This coupling produces spatial dephasing and wall-traction phase anomalies that cannot be mimicked by any constant-complex-viscosity model, even though
the governing problem remains linear and one-dimensional.
Two complementary analytic lenses make the mechanism transparent: a small-
\varepsilon
perturbation formula yielding an explicit first-order correction
to the wall impedance, and a phase-compensation rewrite that conjugates the leading viscous part to a real-coefficient form while isolating the
unavoidable
\varphi^{\prime}
-drift.

Consider an incompressible fluid occupying the half-space
y>0
with a rigid wall at
y=0
oscillating tangentially in the
x
-direction.
Restrict to translationally invariant shear fields

\mathbf{v}(y,t)=(u(y,t),0,0),\qquad u(\cdot,t)\to 0\ \text{as }y\to\infty,

for which the only nonzero strain-rate component is
D_{xy}=\tfrac{1}{2}\partial_{y}u
, hence the shear stress is

\tau_{xy}=2\mu^{*}D_{xy}=\mu^{*}\partial_{y}u
.
Under time-harmonic forcing at angular frequency
\omega>0
,

u(y,t)=\real\{\hat{u}(y)\,e^{i\omega t}\},

the linear momentum balance reduces to the scalar ODE in flux form

-(\mu^{*}(y)\,\hat{u}^{\prime}(y))^{\prime}+i\omega\rho\,\hat{u}(y)=0,\qquad\hat{u}(0)=U_{w},\qquad\hat{u}(y)\to 0\ \text{as }y\to\infty,

(7.1)

where
\rho>0
is (for simplicity) constant density and
U_{w}
is the wall-velocity phasor.
Equation (
7.1
) is the natural insertion point for complex viscosity at fixed frequency:
\mu^{*}
multiplies the strain-rate flux,
and spatial heterogeneity inevitably generates coefficient-gradient couplings in strong form.
We work in the physically passive and analytically coercive regime

\mu^{*}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{+};\mathbb{C}),\qquad\real\mu^{*}(y)\geq\mu_{\min}>0\quad\text{for a.e.\ }y\geq 0.

(7.2)

This is the one-dimensional analogue of Tier I well-posedness: the real part of the viscous power density is uniformly nonnegative and the associated
sesquilinear form is coercive on
H^{1}
after homogenizing the boundary data. To make the decay condition
\hat{u}(y)\to 0
unambiguous, and to match the intended “phase-defect layer” interpretation, we assume that the texture is
localized near the wall. A convenient (and sufficiently general) hypothesis is:

\exists\,y_{\infty}>0\ \text{such that}\ \mu^{*}(y)=\mu_{\infty}^{*}\ \text{for a.e.\ }y\geq y_{\infty},\qquad\real\mu_{\infty}^{*}>0.

(7.3)

Then on
(y_{\infty},\infty)
the ODE has constant coefficients and the solution decays exponentially with rate
\real k_{\infty}
, where

k_{\infty}:=\sqrt{i\omega\rho/\mu_{\infty}^{*}}
is chosen with
\real k_{\infty}>0
.
(One may replace (
7.3
) by
\mu^{*}(y)\to\mu_{\infty}^{*}
with standard asymptotic arguments; we keep the eventually-constant
form to avoid technicalities that are irrelevant to the near-wall mechanism.) If
\mu^{*}(y)\equiv\mu_{0}
is constant (real or complex with
\real\mu_{0}>0
), then (
7.1
) reduces to

-\mu_{0}\hat{u}^{\prime\prime}+i\omega\rho\,\hat{u}=0.

Define

k_{0}:=\sqrt{\frac{i\omega\rho}{\mu_{0}}},\qquad\real k_{0}>0,

(7.4)

where the branch is selected by the decay condition. The unique decaying solution is

\hat{u}_{0}(y)=U_{w}\,e^{-k_{0}y}.

(7.5)

Thus, the decay envelope is a single exponential with length scale
1/\real k_{0}
, and the unwrapped phase is affine in
y
with slope
-\imaginary k_{0}
.
This “single complex wavenumber” structure is rigid: any constant-complex-viscosity model yields
exactly
the profile

\hat{u}(y)=U_{w}e^{-ky}
for some constant
k
with
\real k>0
, hence an affine phase profile and a single penetration depth. We also record the associated (classical) Stokes layer thickness in the Newtonian case
\mu_{0}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}

[
46
,
47
]
:

\delta(\omega):=\sqrt{\frac{2\mu_{0}}{\rho\omega}},\qquad k_{0}=\frac{1+i}{\delta},\qquad\real k_{0}=\frac{1}{\delta},\ \imaginary k_{0}=\frac{1}{\delta}.

In particular, the complex wall shear impedance
\hat{\tau}_{xy}(0)/U_{w}=-\mu_{0}k_{0}
has argument
\pi/4
modulo the sign convention for wall traction.

We now specialize to the phase-only texture class

\mu^{*}(y,\omega)=\mu_{0}(\omega)\,e^{i\varphi(y)},\qquad\mu_{0}(\omega)>0,\qquad\varphi:\mathbb{R}_{+}\to\mathbb{R},

(7.6)

with
\varphi
localized near
y=0
in the sense of (
7.3
) (a “phase-defect wall layer”).
Passivity (
7.2
) becomes the pointwise constraint

\real\mu^{*}(y)=\mu_{0}\cos\varphi(y)\geq\mu_{\min}>0,\quad\text{i.e.}\quad\cos\varphi(y)\geq\mu_{\min}/\mu_{0}\ \text{a.e.},

so
\varphi
may vary strongly but must remain uniformly bounded away from
\pm\pi/2
(mod
2\pi
).
In this class,

\mu^{*\prime}(y)=i\,\mu^{*}(y)\,\varphi^{\prime}(y),

and expanding the flux term in (
7.1
) yields the strong form

\mu^{*}(y)\,\hat{u}^{\prime\prime}(y)+\mu^{*\prime}(y)\,\hat{u}^{\prime}(y)=i\omega\rho\,\hat{u}(y).

(7.7)

Even though
|\mu^{*}|=\mu_{0}
is spatially uniform,
\varphi^{\prime}(y)
enters through the first-order term
\mu^{*\prime}\hat{u}^{\prime}
.
This is the minimal algebraic sense in which phase textures differ from constant-complex-viscosity models: a constant phase
\varphi\equiv\varphi_{0}

can be removed by a global unit-modulus rotation, whereas a
spatially varying
phase changes the operator itself by introducing a drift-like
coupling aligned with the shear localization.

Remark 7.1
(Normal vs. non-normal: what changes in this 1D calibration problem)
.

Let
A=-\partial_{yy}
on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
with Dirichlet data at
y=0
, so
A
is selfadjoint and positive.
For constant viscosity, the harmonic operator is
L_{0}=\mu_{0}A+i\omega\rho\,I
, hence
L_{0}
is normal (indeed, it is a bounded holomorphic
function of a single selfadjoint operator).
Therefore, in this calibration geometry constant complex viscosity produces non-selfadjointness only in the trivial “scalar rotation” sense.

In contrast, when
\mu^{*}
varies in space,
L=-(\mu^{*}\partial_{y})^{\prime}+i\omega\rho
is generically non-normal because multiplication by
\mu^{*}(y)
fails
to commute with differentiation.
In the phase-only class, this failure is controlled precisely by
\varphi^{\prime}(y)
through
\mu^{*\prime}=i\mu^{*}\varphi^{\prime}
.
This one-dimensional model is therefore a literal toy instance of the paper’s operator-level claim:

intrinsic non-normality can arise from the viscous core itself once phase varies in space, even without advection

[
9
]
.

Wall traction, impedance, and the passivity identity.

Define the shear-stress phasor

\hat{\tau}_{xy}(y):=\mu^{*}(y)\,\hat{u}^{\prime}(y).

For the half-space
y>0
, the outward unit normal at
y=0
is
\mathbf{n}=-\mathbf{e}_{y}
, so the tangential traction exerted by the fluid on the wall is

\hat{\tau}_{w}:=(\hat{\bm{\tau}}\mathbf{n})\cdot\mathbf{e}_{x}=-\hat{\tau}_{xy}(0)=-\mu^{*}(0)\,\hat{u}^{\prime}(0).

Introduce the wall impedance-like quantity

Z_{w}(\omega):=\frac{\hat{\tau}_{w}}{U_{w}}.

(7.8)

In oscillatory shear experiments,
\arg Z_{w}(\omega)
is the traction–velocity phase lag at the boundary and is directly measurable

[
46
,
47
]
. A standard complex power identity makes the passivity content explicit. Multiply (
7.1
) by
\overline{\hat{u}}
, integrate on

(0,\infty)
, and integrate by parts (using
\hat{u}(0)=U_{w}
and
\hat{u}(y)\to 0
as
y\to\infty
) to obtain

\hat{\tau}_{w}\,\overline{U_{w}}=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mu^{*}(y)\,|\hat{u}^{\prime}(y)|^{2}\,dy+i\omega\rho\int_{0}^{\infty}|\hat{u}(y)|^{2}\,dy.

(7.9)

Taking real parts yields the dissipative balance

\real\{\hat{\tau}_{w}\,\overline{U_{w}}\}=\int_{0}^{\infty}\real\mu^{*}(y)\,|\hat{u}^{\prime}(y)|^{2}\,dy\ \geq\ 0,

(7.10)

and whenever
U_{w}\neq 0
,

\real Z_{w}(\omega)=\frac{1}{|U_{w}|^{2}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\real\mu^{*}(y)\,|\hat{u}^{\prime}(y)|^{2}\,dy\ \geq\ 0.

(7.11)

Thus, passivity enforces
\real Z_{w}\geq 0
but does not prevent large excursions in
\arg Z_{w}
when the reactive balance is modulated by the texture. For the constant-viscosity baseline (
7.5
), one has

\hat{\tau}_{w,0}=-\mu_{0}\hat{u}_{0}^{\prime}(0)=\mu_{0}k_{0}U_{w},\qquad Z_{w,0}(\omega)=\mu_{0}k_{0},

so in the Newtonian case
\mu_{0}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
,
Z_{w,0}=\mu_{0}(1+i)/\delta
and
\arg Z_{w,0}=\pi/4
.
Taking imaginary parts of (
7.9
) gives the complementary reactive identity

\imaginary\{\hat{\tau}_{w}\,\overline{U_{w}}\}=\omega\rho\int_{0}^{\infty}|\hat{u}(y)|^{2}\,dy+\int_{0}^{\infty}\imaginary\mu^{*}(y)\,|\hat{u}^{\prime}(y)|^{2}\,dy,

(7.12)

which shows explicitly how phase textures can redistribute inertial storage and constitutive storage without changing the sign constraint
(
7.11
).

To isolate the
new effect
in a controlled perturbative regime, let

\varphi(y)=\varepsilon\chi(y),\qquad 0<\varepsilon\ll 1,\qquad\chi\in W^{1,\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{+})\ \text{localized near }y=0,

(7.13)

so that

\mu^{*}(y)=\mu_{0}e^{i\varepsilon\chi(y)}=\mu_{0}\big(1+i\varepsilon\chi(y)\big)+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{2})\quad\text{in }L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{+}).

Seek
\hat{u}=\hat{u}_{0}+\varepsilon\hat{u}_{1}+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{2})
, where
\hat{u}_{0}
is the baseline (
7.5
). Define

\mathcal{L}_{0}\hat{u}:=-\mu_{0}\hat{u}^{\prime\prime}+i\omega\rho\,\hat{u},\qquad k_{0}=\sqrt{\frac{i\omega\rho}{\mu_{0}}},\ \real k_{0}>0.

(7.14)

Collecting
\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon)
terms in (
7.1
) yields

\mathcal{L}_{0}\hat{u}_{1}=i\mu_{0}\,(\chi\,\hat{u}_{0}^{\prime})^{\prime},\qquad\hat{u}_{1}(0)=0,\qquad\hat{u}_{1}(y)\to 0\ \text{as }y\to\infty.

(7.15)

The forcing is the divergence of a localized flux: it vanishes wherever
\chi
is constant and is concentrated inside the defect layer.
Moreover, since
\hat{u}_{0}^{\prime}
is largest near the wall, the forcing is automatically “shear-weighted.”

Green representation.

Let
G_{0}(y,s)
solve
\mathcal{L}_{0}G_{0}(\cdot,s)=\delta(\cdot-s)
on
(0,\infty)
with Dirichlet condition
G_{0}(0,s)=0
and decay as
y\to\infty
.
Then

G_{0}(y,s)=\frac{1}{2\mu_{0}k_{0}}\Big(e^{-k_{0}|y-s|}-e^{-k_{0}(y+s)}\Big),\qquad y,s>0,

(7.16)

and

\hat{u}_{1}(y)=i\mu_{0}\int_{0}^{\infty}G_{0}(y,s)\,(\chi(s)\hat{u}_{0}^{\prime}(s))^{\prime}\,ds.

(7.17)

Expanding the wall traction
\hat{\tau}_{w}=-\mu^{*}(0)\hat{u}^{\prime}(0)
gives

Z_{w}(\omega)=Z_{w,0}(\omega)+\varepsilon Z_{w,1}(\omega)+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{2}),\qquad Z_{w,0}=\mu_{0}k_{0},

with

Z_{w,1}(\omega)=-\mu_{0}\,\frac{\hat{u}_{1}^{\prime}(0)}{U_{w}}+i\mu_{0}k_{0}\,\chi(0).

(7.18)

Differentiating (
7.17
) in
y
and using

\partial_{y}G_{0}(0,s)=\frac{1}{\mu_{0}}e^{-k_{0}s},\qquad s>0,

one obtains

\frac{\hat{u}_{1}^{\prime}(0)}{U_{w}}=i\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-k_{0}s}\,(\chi(s)\hat{u}_{0}^{\prime}(s))^{\prime}\,\frac{ds}{U_{w}}=i\int_{0}^{\infty}\big(-k_{0}\chi^{\prime}(s)+k_{0}^{2}\chi(s)\big)e^{-2k_{0}s}\,ds,

where we used
\hat{u}_{0}^{\prime}(s)=-k_{0}U_{w}e^{-k_{0}s}
.
Substituting into (
7.18
) and integrating by parts (using localization of
\chi
and
\real k_{0}>0
) yields the closed form

Z_{w,1}(\omega)=i\mu_{0}k_{0}^{2}\int_{0}^{\infty}\chi(s)\,e^{-2k_{0}s}\,ds,

(7.19)

i.e.
Z_{w,1}
is (up to
i\mu_{0}k_{0}^{2}
) the Laplace transform of
\chi
evaluated at
2k_{0}
.
Equivalently, one may write (
7.19
) in a form that exhibits the role of
\chi^{\prime}
:

Z_{w,1}(\omega)=\frac{i\mu_{0}k_{0}}{2}\,\chi(0)+\frac{i\mu_{0}k_{0}}{2}\int_{0}^{\infty}\chi^{\prime}(s)\,e^{-2k_{0}s}\,ds,

(7.20)

obtained by a single integration by parts in (
7.19
).
The weighting
e^{-2k_{0}s}
localizes the correction to the classical Stokes layer: only
s\lesssim 1/\real k_{0}
contributes appreciably.

Remark 7.2
(Scaling and what the impedance “sees”)
.

If
\chi
is supported in
[0,\ell]
with
\|\chi\|_{L^{\infty}}\sim 1
, then (
7.19
) yields

|Z_{w,1}(\omega)|\ \lesssim\ \mu_{0}|k_{0}|^{2}\int_{0}^{\ell}e^{-2(\real k_{0})s}\,ds\ \lesssim\ \mu_{0}|k_{0}|^{2}\,\min\{\ell,(\real k_{0})^{-1}\}.

Thus, the impedance correction is controlled by a
Stokes-layer-weighted moment
of the phase defect.
In particular, for very thin defects
\ell\ll(\real k_{0})^{-1}
the impedance correction scales like
\ell
at fixed amplitude (while the interior
profile may still exhibit strong localized dephasing).
This distinction is useful experimentally: wall impedance primarily senses a near-wall
integrated
effect, whereas spatially resolved phase
profiles (or local traction gradients) are more directly sensitive to sharp
\chi^{\prime}
features.

Phase-compensation lens: isolating the unavoidable
\varphi^{\prime}
-drift.

The small-
\varepsilon
expansion isolates the phase-gradient mechanism perturbatively, but it is also useful to exhibit an
exact
rewrite that
(i) removes the unit-modulus factor from the leading viscous flux and (ii) makes transparent which terms are genuinely eliminable by a gauge-like change
of unknown and which are not.
In the pure phase class (
7.6
), define the compensated amplitude

\hat{w}:=e^{i\varphi}\hat{u}\qquad\Longleftrightarrow\qquad\hat{u}=e^{-i\varphi}\hat{w}.

Then

\hat{u}^{\prime}=e^{-i\varphi}(\hat{w}^{\prime}-i\varphi^{\prime}\hat{w}),\qquad\mu^{*}\hat{u}^{\prime}=\mu_{0}(\hat{w}^{\prime}-i\varphi^{\prime}\hat{w}),

so the viscous flux becomes a real-coefficient expression in the compensated variables. Substituting into (
7.1
) yields

-\mu_{0}(\hat{w}^{\prime}-i\varphi^{\prime}\hat{w})^{\prime}+i\omega\rho\,e^{-i\varphi}\hat{w}=0,\qquad\hat{w}(0)=e^{i\varphi(0)}U_{w},\qquad\hat{w}(y)\to 0\ \text{as }y\to\infty.

(7.21)

If
\varphi\in W^{2,\infty}
(or interpreting derivatives in distributions), expanding the viscous term gives

-\mu_{0}(\hat{w}^{\prime}-i\varphi^{\prime}\hat{w})^{\prime}=-\mu_{0}\hat{w}^{\prime\prime}+i\mu_{0}\varphi^{\prime}\hat{w}^{\prime}+i\mu_{0}\varphi^{\prime\prime}\hat{w},

and hence the compensated equation may be written as

-\mu_{0}\hat{w}^{\prime\prime}\;+\;i\mu_{0}\varphi^{\prime}(y)\,\hat{w}^{\prime}\;+\;\Big(i\mu_{0}\varphi^{\prime\prime}(y)+i\omega\rho\,e^{-i\varphi(y)}\Big)\hat{w}\;=\;0.

(7.22)

In this form the leading part is the symmetric elliptic operator
-\mu_{0}\partial_{yy}
, but it is perturbed by a
purely imaginary
first-order drift

i\mu_{0}\varphi^{\prime}\partial_{y}
and by lower-order terms, one of which is phase-modulated inertia
i\omega\rho e^{-i\varphi}\hat{w}
.

The key point is that spatial phase variation enters at
first order
through
\varphi^{\prime}\hat{w}^{\prime}
, and this term is not removable by any global rotation:
it vanishes if and only if
\varphi^{\prime}\equiv 0
a.e.
The transformation
\hat{u}\mapsto\hat{w}=e^{i\varphi}\hat{u}
is unitary on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
and bounded on
H^{1}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
when

\varphi\in W^{1,\infty}
, so it does not alter basic energy well-posedness at the level of coercive estimates.
What it does is separate two effects:

•

a
removable
pointwise complex phase factor multiplying the viscous flux (removed by the compensation);

•

an
unavoidable
commutator effect due to non-commutation of differentiation with multiplication by
e^{-i\varphi}
, captured precisely by

\varphi^{\prime}
(and by
\varphi^{\prime\prime}
at lower order).

Thus, even in the pure phase class where
|\mu^{*}|
is constant and
\mu^{*}
differs from
\mu_{0}
only by a unit complex factor, the operator differs from
the constant-viscosity operator by a genuine first-order term controlled by
\varphi^{\prime}
.
In terms of
\hat{w}
, the wall traction becomes

\hat{\tau}_{w}=-\mu^{*}(0)\hat{u}^{\prime}(0)=-\mu_{0}\big(\hat{w}^{\prime}(0)-i\varphi^{\prime}(0)\hat{w}(0)\big),

and hence

Z_{w}(\omega)=\frac{\hat{\tau}_{w}}{U_{w}}=-\mu_{0}\,\frac{\hat{w}^{\prime}(0)}{U_{w}}\;+\;i\mu_{0}\,\varphi^{\prime}(0)\,\frac{\hat{w}(0)}{U_{w}}=-\mu_{0}e^{i\varphi(0)}\frac{\hat{w}^{\prime}(0)}{\hat{w}(0)}\;+\;i\mu_{0}\,\varphi^{\prime}(0)\,e^{i\varphi(0)}.

(7.23)

This representation makes two points explicit: (i) the impedance depends not only on the value
\varphi(0)
(a pure phase rotation) but also on the

local gradient

\varphi^{\prime}(0)
; and (ii) even if
\varphi(0)=0
(no phase offset at the wall), the gradient term can shift
\arg Z_{w}
through the
additive contribution
i\mu_{0}\varphi^{\prime}(0)
.

For constant viscosity, the profile is necessarily
U_{w}e^{-k_{0}y}
, so the logarithmic derivative
-\hat{u}^{\prime}(y)/\hat{u}(y)
is constant and equals
k_{0}
.
In the compensated equation (
7.22
), the drift term
i\mu_{0}\varphi^{\prime}\hat{w}^{\prime}
forces the logarithmic derivative to vary with
y

whenever
\varphi^{\prime}
is nonzero, so there is no reduction to a single global wavenumber. Equivalently, the phase profile of
\hat{u}
is no longer constrained
to be affine and the decay envelope need not be a single exponential.
This is the precise sense in which spatial phase textures break the classical “one complex number controls everything” structure of Stokes’ second problem
while remaining fully linear.

Takeaway (summary and what is structurally new).

This worked example revisits Stokes’ second problem as a calibration laboratory for constitutive phase textures.
The classical constant-viscosity solution is rigid: the velocity phasor is
\hat{u}_{0}(y)=U_{w}e^{-k_{0}y}
with a single complex wavenumber

k_{0}=\sqrt{i\omega\rho/\mu_{0}}
, so the boundary layer has one penetration depth
1/\real k_{0}
and an affine-in-
y
phase profile with slope
-\imaginary k_{0}
.
In that setting the wall impedance
Z_{w,0}=\mu_{0}k_{0}
has a fixed phase relation to the wall motion (e.g.
\arg Z_{w,0}=\pi/4
for a Newtonian fluid).

In contrast, when viscosity is treated as a spatially resolved complex field
\mu^{*}(y,\omega)
and, in particular, in the phase-only class

\mu^{*}(y)=\mu_{0}e^{i\varphi(y)}
with a localized near-wall defect, the governing operator changes
even though

|\mu^{*}|=\mu_{0}
is uniform.
Expanding the flux form shows that
\mu^{*\prime}\hat{u}^{\prime}=i\mu^{*}\varphi^{\prime}\hat{u}^{\prime}
enters as a genuine first-order coupling: spatial phase variation creates
a drift-like mechanism aligned with the region of largest shear.
Consequently, the classical single-wavenumber picture generically fails: the phase of
\hat{u}(y)
need not be affine, the decay need not be a single exponential,
and the traction phase at the wall can shift in a way that cannot be replicated by choosing a different constant complex viscosity.

Two complementary analyses make the mechanism quantitatively defensible:

•

Passivity and energetic consistency.

Under
\real\mu^{*}\geq\mu_{\min}>0
, the complex power identity yields
\real Z_{w}(\omega)\geq 0
, so phase-texture effects occur within a strictly passive
dissipative regime. The imaginary-part balance shows how
\imaginary\mu^{*}
and inertia redistribute out-of-phase response, enabling excursions in
\arg Z_{w}

without violating dissipation constraints.

•

Perturbative calibration.

For
\varphi=\varepsilon\chi
with
\varepsilon\ll 1
, the first-order correction solves a constant-coefficient forced problem whose forcing is the divergence
of a localized flux
i\mu_{0}(\chi\hat{u}_{0}^{\prime})^{\prime}
. At the wall, the impedance correction admits the explicit closed form (
7.19
), i.e. a Stokes-layer
weighted moment (Laplace transform) of the phase defect.

•

Non-perturbative structural lens.

The exact compensation change
\hat{w}=e^{i\varphi}\hat{u}
removes the unit-modulus factor from the viscous flux but leaves an unavoidable first-order drift

i\mu_{0}\varphi^{\prime}\partial_{y}
(and lower-order terms). Hence any effect persisting after compensation is attributable to
\varphi^{\prime}
and cannot be removed by a
global phase rotation.

The net conclusion is that, even in this simplest boundary-layer geometry with no advection and no geometric singularities, spatial phase variation in a
passive complex viscosity field generates a qualitatively new
linear
mechanism: constitutive phase gradients deform the Stokes layer and produce
traction/impedance phase anomalies that are impossible in constant-viscosity Stokes’ second problem.

7.2
Worked Example II: Oscillatory Couette Flow with Phase-Only Texture and an “Intrinsic Non-Normality without Advection” Lens

Oscillatory Couette flow between parallel plates is deliberately “too simple” from the classical standpoint: the geometry is flat, the kinematics reduce
to a single scalar velocity component, and for constant viscosity the harmonic response is controlled by one complex length scale. Precisely because the

geometric
and
convective
routes to amplification (corners, separation, and Oseen-type non-normality from advection) are absent by
construction (for the one-dimensional ansatz below the convective term vanishes identically) Couette becomes an unusually clean setting in which
to isolate the constitutive mechanism developed in this paper:

even with trivial geometry and strictly linear kinematics, a spatially varying constitutive phase field
\varphi(y)
reshapes the operator geometry
of the viscous core, generating spatially inhomogeneous dephasing and shear localization that cannot be reduced to a single global phase shift.

This worked example is therefore a “null-geometry, no-advection” demonstration: any effect observed here is constitutive.

Consider an incompressible fluid in the slab
y\in(0,H)
with flow in the
x
direction,

\mathbf{v}(y,t)=u(y,t)\,\mathbf{e}_{x},\qquad\nabla\cdot\mathbf{v}=0\ \text{identically}.

The bottom plate is fixed and the top plate oscillates tangentially with prescribed wall velocity

u(0,t)=0,\qquad u(H,t)=\real\{U_{w}e^{i\omega t}\},\qquad\omega>0.

In the strictly linear unsteady-Stokes regime, the
x
-momentum balance reads

\rho\,\partial_{t}u=\partial_{y}\tau_{xy}.

(7.24)

We write time-harmonic phasors

u(y,t)=\real\{\hat{u}(y)e^{i\omega t}\},\qquad\tau_{xy}(y,t)=\real\{\hat{\tau}(y)e^{i\omega t}\},

and insert the constitutive closure at the correct (flux) level:

\hat{\tau}(y)=\mu^{*}(y,\omega)\,\hat{u}^{\prime}(y),\qquad\mu^{*}(y,\omega)=|\mu^{*}(y,\omega)|e^{i\varphi(y,\omega)}.

(7.25)

Then (
7.24
) yields the second-order
flux-form
ODE

-\big(\mu^{*}(y,\omega)\,\hat{u}^{\prime}(y)\big)^{\prime}+i\omega\rho\,\hat{u}(y)=0,\qquad\hat{u}(0)=0,\ \hat{u}(H)=U_{w}.

(7.26)

Throughout this example we assume a uniform dissipation condition (the 1D analogue of the accretivity hypothesis used in the PDE setting):

\real\mu^{*}(y,\omega)\ \geq\ \mu_{\min}>0\quad\text{a.e.\ in }(0,H).

(7.27)

This rules out active/ill-posed rheology and is the minimal hypothesis needed for coercive form methods at fixed
\omega
. To cleanly separate “global phase” effects from
spatially textured
phase effects, we benchmark against

\text{(B0) Newtonian:}\ \mu^{*}\equiv\mu_{0}\in\mathbb{R}_{+},\qquad\text{(B1) global phase:}\ \mu^{*}\equiv\mu_{0}e^{i\varphi_{0}},\qquad\text{(B2) phase texture:}\ \mu^{*}(y)\equiv\mu_{0}e^{i\varphi(y)}.

In (B0) there is no constitutive storage (no complex phase in
\mu^{*}
); any phase lag arises from unsteady inertia and boundary forcing.
In (B1) there is constitutive lag, but the coefficients remain constant, so the harmonic operator is a scalar rotation of a symmetric elliptic operator and
remains normal in the natural
L^{2}
setting. In (B2) the magnitude is held fixed and all heterogeneity enters through the spatially varying phase

\varphi(y)
, forcing non-commutation between multiplication and differentiation. This is the minimal class in which any deviation from the
constant-coefficient picture must be attributed to phase texture rather than magnitude variation.

In the phase-only class

\mu^{*}(y)=\mu_{0}e^{i\varphi(y)},\qquad\mu_{0}>0\ \text{constant (at fixed $\omega$)},

(7.28)

condition (
7.27
) becomes

\real\mu^{*}(y)=\mu_{0}\cos\varphi(y)\geq\mu_{\min}>0\quad\text{a.e.\ in }(0,H).

(7.29)

A convenient sufficient condition is
\|\varphi\|_{L^{\infty}(0,H)}\leq\frac{\pi}{2}-\delta
for some
\delta\in(0,\frac{\pi}{2})
. For functional-analytic clarity, we eliminate the inhomogeneous boundary condition at
y=H
.
Fix any lifting
\hat{u}_{\mathrm{lift}}\in H^{1}(0,H)
such that

\hat{u}_{\mathrm{lift}}(0)=0,\qquad\hat{u}_{\mathrm{lift}}(H)=U_{w}\quad\text{(e.g.\ $\hat{u}_{\mathrm{lift}}(y)=U_{w}y/H$)}.

Write

\hat{u}=\tilde{u}+\hat{u}_{\mathrm{lift}},\qquad\tilde{u}\in H_{0}^{1}(0,H).

(7.30)

Then (
7.26
) becomes a forced homogeneous problem in variational form:

-\big(\mu^{*}\tilde{u}^{\prime}\big)^{\prime}+i\omega\rho\,\tilde{u}=\hat{f},\qquad\tilde{u}\in H_{0}^{1}(0,H),

(7.31)

with forcing

\hat{f}:=\big(\mu^{*}\hat{u}_{\mathrm{lift}}^{\prime}\big)^{\prime}-i\omega\rho\,\hat{u}_{\mathrm{lift}},

(7.32)

interpreted in
H^{-1}(0,H)
under
\mu^{*}\in L^{\infty}(0,H)
(and, in particular,
\hat{f}\in L^{2}(0,H)
if
\mu^{*}\in W^{1,\infty}(0,H)
).
Define the sesquilinear form on
H_{0}^{1}(0,H)

\mathfrak{a}_{\omega}(u,v)=\int_{0}^{H}\mu^{*}(y)\,u^{\prime}(y)\,\overline{v^{\prime}(y)}\,dy+i\omega\rho\int_{0}^{H}u(y)\overline{v(y)}\,dy.

(7.33)

Then

\real\mathfrak{a}_{\omega}(u,u)=\int_{0}^{H}\real(\mu^{*})\,|u^{\prime}|^{2}\,dy\ \geq\ \mu_{\min}\|u^{\prime}\|_{L^{2}(0,H)}^{2}\ \gtrsim\ \|u\|_{H_{0}^{1}(0,H)}^{2},

where the final inequality is Poincaré on
(0,H)
.
Thus, by Lax–Milgram, for each
\hat{f}\in H^{-1}(0,H)
there exists a unique
\tilde{u}\in H_{0}^{1}(0,H)
satisfying

\mathfrak{a}_{\omega}(\tilde{u},v)=\langle\hat{f},v\rangle
for all
v\in H_{0}^{1}(0,H)
.
This emphasizes the intended point: solvability is standard. The novelty lies in the operator geometry and the resulting amplification descriptors.

When
\mu^{*}
is constant (real or complex), (
7.26
) reduces to

-\mu^{*}\,\hat{u}^{\prime\prime}+i\omega\rho\,\hat{u}=0,\qquad\hat{u}(0)=0,\ \hat{u}(H)=U_{w}.

Define

k^{2}:=\frac{i\omega\rho}{\mu^{*}},\qquad\real k>0\ \text{(branch chosen by the boundary-value problem)}.

(7.34)

Then the unique solution is

\hat{u}_{0}(y)=U_{w}\,\frac{\sinh(ky)}{\sinh(kH)}.

(7.35)

In the low-frequency limit
|k|H\ll 1
, (
7.35
) reduces to the classical Couette profile

\hat{u}_{0}(y)\approx U_{w}y/H
. In the inertial regime
|k|H\gtrsim 1
, the profile develops boundary-layer character and a spatially uniform phase
geometry controlled by the single complex parameter
k
.

The complex shear traction at the bottom plate is

\hat{\tau}_{0}(0)=\mu^{*}\,\hat{u}_{0}^{\prime}(0)=\mu^{*}\,U_{w}\,\frac{k}{\sinh(kH)}=U_{w}\,\frac{i\omega\rho}{k}\,\frac{1}{\sinh(kH)},

(7.36)

where the final identity uses
\mu^{*}k^{2}=i\omega\rho
. At the top plate,

\hat{\tau}_{0}(H)=\mu^{*}\,\hat{u}_{0}^{\prime}(H)=\mu^{*}\,U_{w}\,k\,\coth(kH).

In (B1),
\mu^{*}=\mu_{0}e^{i\varphi_{0}}
simply rotates the effective scale
k
and produces a
global
constitutive lag; the spatial phase geometry
remains rigidly controlled by a single
k
. To formalize this rigidity, let
A
denote the Dirichlet Laplacian
Au:=-u^{\prime\prime}
on
L^{2}(0,H)
with domain

D(A)=H^{2}(0,H)\cap H_{0}^{1}(0,H)
. Then
A
is selfadjoint and positive, and for constant
\mu^{*}
the viscous operator is
\mu^{*}A
, which is normal as a
scalar multiple of a selfadjoint operator. Since
I
commutes with
A
, the harmonic operator
\mu^{*}A+i\omega\rho\,I
is also normal. In particular,
for normal operators the resolvent norm is controlled sharply by the spectral distance (and the pseudospectrum coincides with an
\varepsilon
-neighborhood
of the spectrum)
[
19
,
9
]
. This provides the correct foil for the phase-textured case: any departure from this spectrally rigid
behavior must come from spatial phase variation and the resulting non-commutation in the flux-form operator.

In the phase-only class (
7.28
), the homogeneous lifted problem (
7.31
) becomes

-\mu_{0}\big(e^{i\varphi(y)}\tilde{u}^{\prime}(y)\big)^{\prime}+i\omega\rho\,\tilde{u}(y)=\hat{f}\quad\text{in }H^{-1}(0,H),\qquad\tilde{u}\in H_{0}^{1}(0,H),

(7.37)

with uniform accretivity ensured by (
7.29
). Thus, well-posedness follows immediately from the coercive form
(
7.33
). What changes is not solvability but the
operator geometry
: multiplication by
e^{i\varphi(y)}
fails to commute with
differentiation unless
\varphi
is a.e. constant, and this non-commutation is the mechanism behind intrinsic non-normality in the viscous core.

We now define the phase-compensated amplitude

\tilde{u}(y)=e^{-i\varphi(y)}\,w(y).

(7.38)

A direct calculation gives

\tilde{u}^{\prime}=e^{-i\varphi}\big(w^{\prime}-i\varphi^{\prime}w\big),\qquad\mu^{*}\tilde{u}^{\prime}=\mu_{0}\big(w^{\prime}-i\varphi^{\prime}w\big).

Substituting into (
7.37
) yields the compensated equation (in distributions)

-\mu_{0}\big(w^{\prime}-i\varphi^{\prime}w\big)^{\prime}+i\omega\rho\,e^{-i\varphi}\,w=\hat{f},\qquad w\in H_{0}^{1}(0,H),

(7.39)

where
w(0)=w(H)=0
since
\tilde{u}\in H_{0}^{1}
and multiplication by
e^{\pm i\varphi}
preserves homogeneous Dirichlet data (e.g. for

\varphi\in W^{1,\infty}
). Two structural features are now explicit:

1.

The leading viscous flux coefficient is
real
(
\mu_{0}
),

2.

The phase texture survives as an
irreducible first-order coupling
through
\varphi^{\prime}
(a covariant-derivative structure),
together with a spatial modulation of the inertial shift through
e^{-i\varphi}
.

In particular, no global phase rotation can remove
\varphi^{\prime}
unless
\varphi
is a.e. constant. Since
(\mu^{*})^{\prime}(y)=i\mu^{*}(y)\varphi^{\prime}(y)
in the
phase-only class, a natural dimensionless Couette phase-gradient parameter is

\Pi_{\varphi}:=H\,\|\varphi^{\prime}\|_{L^{\infty}(0,H)}.

(7.40)

Heuristically, if
\varphi
changes by
\Delta\varphi
across a layer of thickness
\ell
, then
\Pi_{\varphi}\sim H(\Delta\varphi/\ell)
:

\Pi_{\varphi}\ll 1
corresponds to slowly varying phase, whereas
\Pi_{\varphi}\gtrsim 1
corresponds to sharp phase texture and strong commutator effects.
For sharp interfaces (piecewise constant
\varphi
),
\Pi_{\varphi}
is replaced by jump data together with interface flux continuity (discussed below).

For a strong (differential-operator) realization, assume
\mu^{*}\in W^{1,\infty}(0,H)
(equivalently,
\varphi\in W^{1,\infty}(0,H)
in the
phase-only class) and define on
L^{2}(0,H)

A_{\varphi}u:=-(\mu^{*}(y)u^{\prime})^{\prime},\qquad D(A_{\varphi})=H^{2}(0,H)\cap H_{0}^{1}(0,H).

(7.41)

(Equivalently, one may take
D(A_{\varphi})=\{u\in H_{0}^{1}(0,H):(\mu^{*}u^{\prime})^{\prime}\in L^{2}(0,H)\}
, which coincides with
H^{2}\cap H_{0}^{1}
under

\mu^{*}\in W^{1,\infty}
.) The oscillatory operator is the shifted operator

\mathcal{L}_{\varphi}:=A_{\varphi}+i\omega\rho\,I,\qquad D(\mathcal{L}_{\varphi})=D(A_{\varphi}).

(7.42)

Its
L^{2}
-adjoint is

A_{\varphi}^{\dagger}v=-(\overline{\mu^{*}(y)}\,v^{\prime})^{\prime},\qquad\mathcal{L}_{\varphi}^{\dagger}=A_{\varphi}^{\dagger}-i\omega\rho\,I,\qquad D(\mathcal{L}_{\varphi}^{\dagger})=H^{2}(0,H)\cap H_{0}^{1}(0,H).

(7.43)

Since
i\omega\rho I
is a scalar multiple of the identity, it commutes with everything, hence

[\mathcal{L}_{\varphi},\mathcal{L}_{\varphi}^{\dagger}]=[A_{\varphi},A_{\varphi}^{\dagger}],\qquad[A,B]:=AB-BA.

Thus, any non-normality is intrinsic to the viscous core (the variable complex coefficient) and is independent of the inertial shift.

Proposition 7.1

(Intrinsic non-normality from spatial phase variation (no-advection Couette))
.

Assume the phase-only class (
7.28
)–(
7.29
) and
\varphi\in W^{3,\infty}(0,H)
.
If
\varphi^{\prime}
is not identically zero (equivalently,
\varphi
is not a.e. constant), then
\mathcal{L}_{\varphi}
is not normal on
L^{2}(0,H)
:

[\mathcal{L}_{\varphi},\mathcal{L}_{\varphi}^{\dagger}]\neq 0\quad\text{on }C_{c}^{\infty}(0,H).

Conversely, if
\varphi
is constant a.e., then
\mathcal{L}_{\varphi}
is normal (indeed diagonalizable in the sine basis).

Proof.

Let
a(y):=\mu^{*}(y)=\mu_{0}e^{i\varphi(y)}
. Since the shift
i\omega\rho I
commutes with everything,

[\mathcal{L}_{\varphi},\mathcal{L}_{\varphi}^{\dagger}]=[A_{\varphi},A_{\varphi}^{\dagger}]
.
On
u\in C_{c}^{\infty}(0,H)
, one may expand

A_{\varphi}u=-(au^{\prime})^{\prime}=-au^{\prime\prime}-a^{\prime}u^{\prime},\qquad A_{\varphi}^{\dagger}u=-(\overline{a}u^{\prime})^{\prime}=-\overline{a}\,u^{\prime\prime}-\overline{a}^{\prime}\,u^{\prime}.

A direct computation shows that the commutator is a third-order differential operator whose leading term is

[A_{\varphi},A_{\varphi}^{\dagger}]u=2\big(a\overline{a}^{\prime}-\overline{a}a^{\prime}\big)\,u^{(3)}\ +\ \text{(lower-order terms involving $a^{\prime},a^{\prime\prime},a^{(3)}$)}.

(7.44)

In the phase-only class
a=\mu_{0}e^{i\varphi}
one computes

a^{\prime}=i\mu_{0}e^{i\varphi}\varphi^{\prime},\qquad\overline{a}^{\prime}=-i\mu_{0}e^{-i\varphi}\varphi^{\prime},\qquad a\overline{a}^{\prime}-\overline{a}a^{\prime}=-2i\mu_{0}^{2}\,\varphi^{\prime}.

Thus, the coefficient of
u^{(3)}
in (
7.44
) is
-4i\mu_{0}^{2}\varphi^{\prime}
.
If
\varphi^{\prime}\not\equiv 0
, choose
u\in C_{c}^{\infty}(0,H)
supported where
\varphi^{\prime}
does not vanish and with
u^{(3)}\not\equiv 0
there; then the
leading term is nonzero, hence the commutator is nonzero and
\mathcal{L}_{\varphi}
is not normal.
Conversely, if
\varphi
is constant a.e., then
a
is constant and
A_{\varphi}
is a constant-coefficient Dirichlet Sturm–Liouville operator,
diagonalizable by the sine basis; hence
A_{\varphi}
and
A_{\varphi}^{\dagger}
commute and
\mathcal{L}_{\varphi}
is normal.
∎

Remark 7.3
(Minimal regularity versus explicit commutator expansions)
.

The explicit commutator expansion above is stated under
\varphi\in W^{3,\infty}
to justify differentiating coefficients to third order.
At lower regularity (e.g.
\varphi\in W^{1,\infty}
so
a\in W^{1,\infty}
), one may instead work with the m-sectorial form realization and interpret
non-normality via form commutators on a core; the qualitative conclusion is unchanged: non-constant phase texture breaks normality because
multiplication by
e^{i\varphi}
fails to commute with differentiation.

7.2.1
Exact solvability for layered phase textures via transfer matrices and flux continuity.

Couette geometry has no corners, no separation points, and (in the no-advection linearization) no convective non-normality channel.
Proposition
7.1
therefore isolates a purely constitutive mechanism: spatial phase variation alone suffices to destroy
normality of the viscous core, independent of the inertial shift
i\omega\rho I
. In this setting, eigenvalues alone are not reliable amplification
descriptors; resolvent norms and pseudospectra are the appropriate tools even in 1D
[
9
]
. The analytically cleanest textures are layered (piecewise constant) profiles, which can be viewed as sharp limits of thin phase-gradient layers.
Let
0<y_{c}<H
and define

\varphi(y)=\varphi_{1}\ \text{for }y\in(0,y_{c}),\qquad\varphi(y)=\varphi_{2}\ \text{for }y\in(y_{c},H),\qquad\mu_{j}:=\mu_{0}e^{i\varphi_{j}}.

On each layer, the (homogeneous) flux-form equation reduces to a constant-coefficient ODE

-\mu_{j}\hat{u}_{j}^{\prime\prime}+i\omega\rho\,\hat{u}_{j}=0,\qquad k_{j}^{2}=\frac{i\omega\rho}{\mu_{j}},\qquad\real k_{j}>0,

where the branch is chosen to ensure the standard decay/energy sign conventions.

The correct transmission conditions follow by integrating the flux-form equation across
y=y_{c}
(equivalently, by enforcing the weak formulation
with test functions supported near the interface):

\hat{u}_{1}(y_{c})=\hat{u}_{2}(y_{c}),\qquad\mu_{1}\hat{u}_{1}^{\prime}(y_{c})=\mu_{2}\hat{u}_{2}^{\prime}(y_{c}).

(7.45)

That is: continuity of velocity and continuity of complex shear traction (flux). These conditions are exactly what is lost if one replaces the
divergence-form operator by
\mu^{*}\partial_{yy}
.

State-space form and transfer matrices.

Introduce the state vector

X(y):=\begin{pmatrix}\hat{u}(y)\\
